10 The Agent Harness

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why must the decision to continue an agent live outside the model that proposes its next step?

A single model invocation succeeds or fails on its own, but an autonomous agent runs across a horizon of many turns, and per-step errors compound with every turn. Over dozens of sequential tool interactions, a malformed call, a stale observation, or an unhandled exception can carry an unsupervised trajectory into a failure or a loop that no single step reveals. The agent harness is the runtime component that owns this loop: it schedules model calls, enforces the rules for moving between states, tracks task budgets, and holds sensitive actions for human approval. A model can be asked to pause, wait, or stay within an allowance, but a request it honors on almost every turn still fails across a long run, so the decision to continue must rest with deterministic supervision outside the model. Engineering the harness turns stochastic token generation into task execution that stays bounded, interruptible, and accountable across the whole horizon.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain why continuation is decided outside the model, and design the runtime-owned trajectory record of plans, grants, consumption, and obligations
  • Construct guarded state transitions that keep stale observations out of the next turn while preserving unresolved effects
  • Calculate how long a control request waits for a turn boundary, and apply pause, cancel, or kill without dispatching incomplete output
  • Explain which resources an asynchronous wait releases and which the trajectory still owns and pays for
  • Design an approval gate with expiry and dispatch-time rechecks, and size the resources its held trajectories retain
  • Allocate shared model-call service by weighted deficit round-robin while keeping provider quotas separate from trajectory budgets
  • Apply bounded-liability admission across a trajectory’s turn, token, time, and dollar budgets, and select a ceiling by cost per accepted task
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

10.1 Supervising Continuation

The horizon exposure, bounded by a harness that keeps control outside the model.

Consider the connection-pool repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) at the moment it produces candidate patch r18. A test job is actively running against a fixed snapshot of that revision when an operator requests a pause for manual inspection. Simultaneously, an independent interactive task queues for model service, and the repair trajectory approaches its allocated budget. Even if the test job finishes cleanly and the next model reply is syntactically flawless, neither event answers whether the repair should proceed right now. That continuation decision depends on operational conditions outside the model: the operator’s pending signal, current task states, shared cluster capacity, and the allowance remaining once in-flight work is accounted for.

The runtime components examined so far each address part of this operational environment. The tool layer tracks job handles and operation repeat contracts, the sandbox isolates execution and bounds workspace lifetimes, and the security checks test each dispatch against its confidentiality and provenance labels and bind each approval to the exact action it covers. Each of these mechanisms, however, operates at the boundary of a single invocation, resource, or decision; none possesses the context or authority to decide whether the trajectory as a whole may take another step. That decision cannot be delegated to the model either. An agent instructed to self-regulate (to pause on request, or to halt before exceeding an allowance) evaluates that rule afresh on every turn. Because of horizon compounding (equation 1.1), even a high per-turn compliance rate decays across an extended run, so a constraint the model honors on almost every turn is still violated in a predictable share of long trajectories. Operational control also requires state that cannot live in an ephemeral context window. An operator needs immediate status without reconstructing the conversation transcript; a scheduler must distinguish between work waiting on background jobs and work eligible to run; and the dispatch path must enforce security grants and token allowances before invoking downstream tools. All three operations demand a single, authoritative account of the trajectory that an unstructured message list cannot maintain.

The agent harness provides this missing supervisory layer by taking ownership of the execution loop for an individual trajectory. The agent proposes candidate actions; the harness determines whether each step runs immediately, yields to other work, or halts, and it enforces those decisions against an execution record that only the runtime can modify. This is trajectory-level encapsulation (principle 10). The runtime governs each model call as one step of a whole trajectory, and the trajectory advances only through transitions and admissions that the runtime’s guards permit. The reason is that the trajectory cannot be relied on to yield, to stop, or to notice that it has stopped making progress. Invariant closure (principle 1) says that bounds belong below the model; this principle names the unit those bounds must cover, the whole trajectory rather than the single call. In the harness, the principle takes the form of two working rules. First, every outbound model call or tool dispatch must pass an explicit admission check (active operator signals, capability grants, shared capacity, and remaining budget) at the instant it would execute. Second, in-flight work stays accountable: no pause signal, timeout, or cancellation proves that running work has stopped or that its cost is settled, so every outstanding operation keeps a named owner until evidence settles it.

The record described in this chapter lives in host memory, while making its guarantees survive host failure is the work of durable execution (Durable Execution). Within a live host, the harness develops in the order these supervisory decisions arise across a repair turn: separating the control path from the bare execution loop (section 10.2), maintaining an authoritative account of the trajectory (section 10.3), and enforcing guarded rules for how that account may change (section 10.4). Operators then need a way to stop or redirect active work (section 10.5), while long tool jobs and human reviews need waits that keep supervision intact (section 10.6, section 10.7). Across concurrent trajectories, shared model service must be divided fairly (section 10.8), and across many turns, cumulative consumption must be bounded (section 10.9). Seeing why those guarantees require a dedicated runtime component starts with what breaks when the r18 repair turn runs inside an unmediated loop.

10.2 The Loop and the Harness

When the r18 repair agent runs inside a bare execution loop, an operator pause during the test suite or an unconstrained retry cycle immediately exposes the lack of an external control plane. A control plane is a path, separate from the one that performs model calls and tool work, that decides whether that work may proceed. An unmanaged loop treats model completions as direct execution instructions; a harness treats them as untrusted proposals, evaluating every action against explicit admission policies, budget constraints, and external supervisory signals.

10.2.1 The anatomy of an unmanaged loop

The unmediated while-loop of An Unmediated Agent Loop is the pattern most prototype agents still use, popularized by ReAct (Yao et al. 2023): invoke a foundation model, dispatch the tools requested in its completion, append the results to the message history, and repeat until the model emits a stopping condition. A single turn spans six phases, from context assembly to assessment against the task contract, and several of those phases must wait on external tools or human reviewers. What the prototype loop lacks for production operation is a place for those phases’ decisions to live, because it confines all operational state to the message list and the local call stack of the client process.

↩︎ The six-phase turn lifecycle, including the phases that pause a turn for tools and approvals, is established in The six phases of a turn.

Confining control state to the message list and call stack leaves three operational paths with nowhere to land. When an operator tries to pause a run during a long test suite, the runtime has no interface to receive the command. When cumulative token consumption exhausts the allocated budget, the loop cannot withhold the next model call without terminating abruptly and abandoning in-flight work. When the model proposes a tool invocation, the call executes immediately under ambient authority, without policy checks or human review. Although a supervised harness may also execute as a loop or coroutine, it wraps that loop in an explicit state machine whose guards govern every continuation path, filtering each proposal before any tool call is admitted (figure 1).

In an unmanaged system, execution context is scattered across disparate subsystems: host operating systems track local processes, remote clusters hold asynchronous job handles, storage systems manage workspace leases, and identity providers track credential expirations. Panel (b) consolidates these responsibilities into a single runtime-owned record for each trajectory. Operators, cluster schedulers, and dispatch paths all consult this authoritative record before initiating state changes. While a system may pass budget constraints to the model in the prompt and the model may cooperate with pause requests, actual enforcement remains strictly with the runtime. Relying on model cooperation violates invariant closure; the runtime itself must guarantee that system safety invariants hold under all execution paths (principle 1).

Definition 10.1: Agent harness

Agent harness is the runtime component that owns the agent loop for one trajectory, assembling each model call, interpreting the reply, deciding whether and when each proposed tool call is dispatched, and keeping the record through which outside components inspect, pause, bound, or stop continuation.

  1. Significance: It keeps continuation decisions independent of model cooperation while preserving the grants, consumption, and outstanding work those decisions must respect.
  2. Distinction: The tool layer mediates calls and tracks their outcomes, and the sandbox bounds execution under its policy. The harness composes those mechanisms with control requests, waiting, scheduling, and lifetime budgets.
  3. Common pitfall: Keeping status and pending obligations only in prompt text or inaccessible execution-local state, so a separate control path cannot inspect them or prevent further dispatch.

10.2.2 One turn under supervision

In a single turn of the r18 repair workflow, the harness mediates every step from the moment the trajectory becomes eligible for another model call, through the model’s proposal to run the regression test against candidate r18, to an operator’s pause and subsequent resume (table 1). Across that turn, lifecycle states record whether the trajectory is eligible for continuation (READY), actively querying the model (CALLING_MODEL), awaiting tool completion (WAITING_TOOL), or suspended by an operator (PAUSED).

Three features of the trace recur through the rest of the chapter. First, every row that admits work reserves before it sends. Affordability cannot be judged from settled charges alone, because the running test job may still bill and the proposed call may generate up to its full output allowance. The harness therefore reserves each operation’s worst-case liability at admission and reconciles reported usage afterward. Notebook 10.2 later prices a call that a check of settled spend admits and this reservation test refuses. Second, the pause and the test result are independent events. The pause stops new admission without stopping the job, and the job’s result settles the job’s outstanding charge without resuming the turn. Third, the reply’s content never acts on its own. A truncated or canceled reply yields nothing to dispatch, a claim of completion triggers an independent check rather than ending the task, and an action that policy requires a human to review waits in a dedicated hold with its own expiry. Each of these needs runtime state that the message list does not have.

The trace also separates the decision to continue from the resources an agent holds. While the repair trajectory sits in PAUSED or WAITING_TOOL, it keeps its workspace, its cached repository state, and its running test job, but it does not hold a worker thread or a place in the queue for model calls. Because the harness, not the repair loop, decides when the trajectory next calls the model, an interactive trajectory can take the next model-call slot without waiting for the repair to give it up. Which resources a wait releases, and which it keeps, is the subject of supervised waiting (section 10.6); how model calls are divided among trajectories is the subject of scheduling (section 10.8). Before the harness can enforce any of these transitions, waits, or schedules, however, it needs an authoritative data structure where that operational state lives.

10.3 The Trajectory Record

When an operator issues a pause request, the conversation transcript cannot answer the questions the harness must resolve. The transcript records what the user and model said, but runtime decisions require knowing the state of the underlying system. Specifically, the harness must know which candidate revision is under test, whether a background validation job has finished, how much compute budget remains once outstanding asynchronous tasks are accounted for, and whether any external side effects are still in flight. Simultaneously, the cluster scheduler must distinguish a trajectory blocked on a container execution from one actively waiting for its next inference turn. None of these operational facts belong in the prompt. Resolving them requires runtime-owned control state that the harness can inspect, mutate, and enforce directly without relying on the foundation model to reconstruct its own execution history.

Definition 10.2: Trajectory record

Trajectory record is the per-trajectory control state owned and maintained by the harness. It encapsulates identity and task scope, lifecycle state, resource limits and financial accounting, security grants and workspace leases, pending asynchronous operations, and references to prompt context, the live execution plan, container environments, and verified evidence.

  1. Significance: It provides the harness with an authoritative, centralized structure to inspect and govern the entire trajectory, tracking resource consumption, pending asynchronous operations, and security grants across inference calls.
  2. Distinction: While the conversation transcript records user-model message exchanges, the trajectory record tracks the operational state required to govern execution across multiple turns. A live in-memory record represents active control state, not a durable audit log or proof that external claims are verified.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a model’s generated text or self-reported progress as an authoritative update to consumed budget, granted capabilities, tool execution results, or task completion.

Table 2 organizes these fields by the supervisory decisions they support. The table is a logical schema. A distributed runtime may partition these field groups across different storage backends, for example keeping ephemeral context references in memory while persisting cost accounts to an external store, provided each group retains a single authoritative writer and a well-defined update protocol.

Two questions about a running operation must be answered separately: whether its result may still inform the model, and whether the harness still owes anything for it. The first is observation eligibility, which defines whether the output of an operation is permitted to enter the prompt context for the next model invocation. The second is an outstanding obligation, and the two can come apart. A tool call can lose observation eligibility (when an invocation step times out, or the user cancels a subtask) even while the remote execution continues running in a backend container. To prevent orphaned side effects and resource leaks, the operation table tracks every logical operation across its entire lifecycle: transport retries, incoming execution evidence, and the specific subsystem responsible for final settlement.

The trajectory record manages this lifecycle through two versioning counters. While record_version increments on every serialized update to provide optimistic concurrency control, the control epoch (control_epoch) tracks the specific planning and invocation cycle against which proposals and observations are validated. The epoch does not advance on every turn; it advances when the harness withdraws a continuation’s claim on outstanding results, as when a wait deadline expires, an operator interrupts, or a plan revision supersedes pending work. Each call records the epoch current at its registration, so an ordinary result from this turn’s dispatch still matches. Incrementing the epoch invalidates in-flight observations registered under the earlier epoch without breaking the underlying operation’s idempotency contract. As a result, the harness continues tracking background tasks and their external side effects even after the agent has stopped waiting for their immediate output.

↩︎ The operation idempotency contract and retry semantics are established in Retrying Uncertain Actions.

A parallel discipline governs resource accounting. The harness separates settled usage (actual_consumption) from provisional allocations (outstanding_reservations), which represent admitted operations whose compute or financial costs have not yet been finalized. The harness withholds a dedicated finalization reserve from general exploratory steps. This reserve guarantees that the runtime retains sufficient budget to execute mandated cleanup tasks, release sandboxes, and run deterministic verification suites before the trajectory terminates. Wall-clock deadlines operate independently of token limits, because waiting on remote containers or human reviewers consumes real time even when token generation is idle. Section 10.9 formalizes this admission test; the trajectory record provides the live metrics required to enforce it.

These lifecycle and accounting guarantees unite under one working rule, the settlement rule. It is the trajectory-level form of settlement before retry (principle 8). That principle says a timeout does not establish whether an effect occurred, so a blind retry is unsafe; the settlement rule adds that losing interest in a result does not release the effect’s charge or its owner either. Under this rule, withdrawing an operation’s observation eligibility, whether through a timeout, a plan revision, an operator pause, or trajectory cancellation, never removes the operation from the operation table or releases its budget reservation. Only authenticated, correlated evidence can settle an outcome, confirm completion, or release reserved budget. Until that proof arrives, the outstanding obligation retains a designated settlement owner responsible for reconciling its ultimate state.

10.3.1 Update authority and referenced state

If a model’s output could directly update its own budget balance, grant set, or step status, a single hallucinated or manipulative reply could reset the spending meter or mark an unrun test complete. Only the harness therefore holds write authority over the trajectory record. While the model can propose plan changes, tool calls, or completion assertions, the harness treats these outputs strictly as unverified proposals that must pass admission checks before the runtime commits them to the record. Provider billing APIs and tool execution metrics supply authoritative consumption figures, cryptographically signed or authenticated tool events supply operational evidence, and control APIs supply verified operator intent. Because this control state stays separate from prompt text, an agent may receive read-only notifications of its remaining budget without ever gaining the ability to change the meter.

To keep control operations lightweight and prevent multi-megabyte state copies during routine scheduler queries, the trajectory record stores references (content hashes, storage URIs, and job identifiers) rather than deep-copying full prompt contexts, workspace filesystems, or observation payloads. However, these references represent real underlying resource commitments. A paused or waiting trajectory continues to consume resources across distributed subsystems: KV-cache allocations in inference engines, isolated container environments and disk volumes, and open network sockets. The trajectory record holds the authoritative handles to these external assets and signals lifecycle transitions to their respective managers.

These in-memory references and handles hold only while the host process remains alive. Preserving this control state across process crashes and ensuring that record updates remain strictly serialized with respect to external side effects requires write-ahead logging and durable execution protocols. A dedicated durable-log offset in the control record binds the volatile runtime state to its persistent backing log.

↪ Committing each admitted operation and its reservation to durable storage before dispatch is developed in Durable Intent Before Dispatch.

10.3.2 The live plan

Once the repair agent generates candidate r18 and dispatches a targeted unit test in a background container, a static candidate graph or a checklist in prompt text can no longer represent the state of the work. It cannot separate the edit that produced r18, the test currently in flight against that revision, and the broader integration suite still waiting on the test result. Runtime execution therefore demands a live plan, a dependency-ordered directed acyclic graph of execution steps that explicitly separates proposed actions from operations currently in flight and evidence already verified. Each step in the plan defines a unique identifier, explicit prerequisite steps or evidence dependencies, the target candidate artifact and operating conditions to which it applies, and its current execution status. When the harness admits a step, it binds that step directly to an entry in the operation table. When a step completes, the harness links it to the immutable evidence supporting that completion, never to an unverified checkbox emitted in model text.

⇄ Contrast: The revisable candidate plan established in Revising Candidate Plans tracks only unexecuted candidate operations and their supporting evidence; the live plan also records operations in flight.

While the test runner executes in its background container, the live plan records the step as in flight rather than optimistically assuming success. When the runner completes, a passing status validates only that targeted test against candidate r18 under those exact container flags; the remaining verification steps and final acceptance criteria remain unfulfilled. An operator’s pause request arriving mid-test acts as an independent supervisory event, interrupting further dispatch without corrupting the historical inputs or the validity of the ongoing test execution.

When incoming observations demonstrate that a strategy is failing, the model may propose a plan revision to alter downstream tasks. Before adopting any revised plan, the harness validates it against the overarching task contract, current evidence dependencies, remaining budget, and security grants. Adopting the revision advances the plan revision counter and invalidates pending, undispatched proposals and held approvals whose underlying assumptions have changed. Existing verified evidence remains valid under its original operating conditions. The harness synchronizes plan adoption with the tool dispatcher to prevent race conditions where a stale proposal might pass admission against a superseded plan.

Now consider what happens if a plan revision modifies the source code while the targeted test for candidate r18 is still running in the background. The active container job is testing candidate r18, not the new revision. Relabeling the step in the plan cannot convert those eventual test results into evidence for the modified code. Deleting the step from the plan likewise does not abort the remote job, reverse its external side effects, or automatically refund its compute reservation. Instead, the harness keeps the active operation in the operation table under its original settlement owner, blocks downstream steps whose prerequisites are no longer satisfied, and evaluates the replacement work through a fresh admission check. Undoing an external effect requires compensation, a later action that offsets the earlier one (Semantic compensation), not a retroactive edit to plan text.

By maintaining an explicit, dependency-tracked live plan, the trajectory record delineates what the agent has proposed, what the harness has admitted to the execution plane, and what obligations remain outstanding. The harness must therefore govern how this record evolves, so that it stays consistent across concurrent tool events, asynchronous worker callbacks, and operator interruptions. It does so by binding all lifecycle mutations to formal, guarded state transitions.

10.4 Trajectory States

Keeping the trajectory record consistent across asynchronous callbacks and timeouts becomes critical when a tool result arrives after the turn that requested it has already moved on. Suppose the wait deadline for candidate r18’s test suite elapses before a result arrives, so the harness records the outcome as unknown and proceeds; one minute later, the test service returns a success report. Both statements reflect reality: the outcome was indeed unknown when the deadline expired, and the tests ultimately passed. The failure occurs if the harness splices that late result into an obsolete turn as though the model had possessed the evidence when selecting its next action. This defect, known as observation crossing, conflates the asynchronous physical history of an operation with the causal evidence admitted to a particular continuation. Preventing it requires two mechanisms: guarded transitions that decide when a continuation may advance, and an epoch fence that decides which late results may still inform it (section 10.4.2).

A state machine with guarded transitions1 makes every such admission explicit. Beyond resolving observation races, guarded transitions prevent scheduling anomalies, such as mistaking a trajectory held for human approval as idle and erroneously dispatching another model call. The runtime control plane, rather than generated model output, governs all state transitions. Table 3 defines this supervisory state machine, organized around five live states, a finalizing state through which every exit must pass, and three terminal absorbing outcomes.

1 Guarded Transition: Harel’s statecharts (Harel 1987) labeled each transition with the event that triggers it and a condition that must hold for it to fire, and added hierarchy and concurrency so that reactive controllers stayed readable as their state counts grew. The harness uses the flat core of that formalism: an event proposes an edge, and the guard, evaluated against the record at that moment, decides whether it fires. Keeping the guard on the edge, rather than in whichever handler happens to receive the event, is what lets one table govern every path into CALLING_MODEL.

Harel, David. 1987. “Statecharts: A Visual Formalism for Complex Systems.” Science of Computer Programming 8 (3): 231–74. https://doi.org/10.1016/0167-6423(87)90035-9.

The normal operational cycle follows READY → CALLING_MODEL → WAITING_TOOL → READY, tracing through the five live states and guarded transitions shown in figure 2. Proposals requiring review divert into AWAITING_APPROVAL. Pause commands transition to PAUSED exclusively from READY. All termination triggers (completion requests, cancellations, or exhausted budgets) route through FINALIZING before reaching the absorbing terminal states (COMPLETED, FAILED, CANCELED). During execution, a model response may produce no admissible action, request task completion, or propose an action requiring operator approval. While the normalized invocation status defined in Normalized invocation status envelope fixes how the runtime processes individual call outcomes, table 4 specifies how each outcome shifts the overall trajectory state. Two names appear in both vocabularies with different meanings: a reply status of COMPLETED or CANCELED describes one model call, while the terminal states of the same names describe the whole trajectory. A completed reply ends a call, not the task. Only a reply whose status is COMPLETED proceeds to tool candidate validation. The harness must never convert a malformed, truncated, or canceled partial stream into an actionable tool invocation only because a parser managed to salvage partial arguments from the payload.

Action approval introduces a controlled detour to this cycle. An approved action transitions from AWAITING_APPROVAL to WAITING_TOOL only when its active dispatch guards pass; recording operator approval does not automatically execute the tool. Conversely, a denial, expiration, or operator withdrawal emits a refusal observation back to READY.

10.4.1 Transition guards and the admission check

A trajectory sitting in READY can still be unsafe to advance if an operator pause arrived a millisecond earlier, a credential expired while the trajectory waited in the queue, or an unresolved prior operation still blocks a dependent mutation. Navigating a legal edge in the state machine is therefore only a necessary condition for progress, not an unconditional right to execute. Before the runtime admits new work, whether invoking a model or dispatching a tool, it evaluates a set of predicates known as the admission check: no pending control hold may be active, the context, credentials, and any required action approvals must remain valid, upstream data dependencies must be resolved, and both deadline slack and downstream service capacity must be confirmed. The runtime must also reserve the operation’s bounded financial liability from the task budget without encroaching on the dedicated finalization reserve (section 10.9), and for tool calls it must verify that arguments are structurally complete. Because system state changes continuously, this evaluation holds only at the instant it executes; an approval granted seconds earlier or a scheduling decision made on a previous tick does not carry forward.

The admission check and its state updates execute as a single atomic transaction. Protected by the trajectory record lock,2 the harness evaluates the guard predicates, reserves the required liability, registers the outgoing invocation or attempt ID, marks the expected observation as eligible, and commits the state transition. Releasing the record lock before network transmission, however, opens a brief window before the bytes leave the host in which an operator pause or cancellation can commit. To close that window without holding the record lock across slow network I/O, the runtime passes the admitted request to a release gate that serializes the final release-or-withhold decision with control updates. This gate rechecks current controls and the existing admission without reserving liability twice, withholding transmission if a stop arrived after the record lock was released. Together, these steps enforce the register-before-send rule. Registering state before dispatch ensures that even an immediate response never arrives before the local record exists to receive it. Conversely, transmitting requests strictly outside the critical section prevents slow network I/O, stalled inference endpoints, or lagging tool services from blocking concurrent control actions, scheduler ticks, or arriving results from other workers.

2 Record Lock as a Monitor: Hoare’s monitors (Hoare 1974) bundled shared data with the only procedures allowed to touch it and ran those procedures one at a time, so every invariant on the data could be checked at procedure entry and exit. The trajectory record is a monitor in this sense: controllers, schedulers, and result handlers all reach it through short guarded procedures. The register-before-send rule adds one constraint for a record whose procedures front slow remote services: no procedure waits on a model or tool call while holding the lock.

Hoare, Charles Antony Richard. 1974. “Monitors: An Operating System Structuring Concept.” Communications of the ACM 17 (10): 549–57. https://doi.org/10.1145/355620.361161.

External control requests acquire this same monitor lock, so stops and admissions are strictly serialized under an admission-order rule. If a pause, cancellation, or kill command commits before an admission check runs, it immediately blocks new outbound work. If admission commits first, the operation becomes owned work with a registered identity and reservation, and a later control cannot erase or refund that reservation on intent alone. Although the release gate can still withhold a definitely unsent attempt, settling that attempt requires authoritative evidence that release did not occur and that no remote liability remains. A volatile withheld flag does not survive a host crash, and once a packet may have left the host, a cancellation request cannot establish not_sent without remote evidence. Tool-side idempotency and stale-lease fencing therefore still require the endpoint contracts detailed in Operation identity and endpoint contracts.

To coordinate concurrent state access without heavyweight distributed locks, the harness pairs optimistic concurrency validation with epoch fencing. The record_version field detects competing updates at the point of commit, implementing the validation step of optimistic concurrency control (Kung and Robinson 1981). In parallel, increments to control_epoch fence out stale asynchronous responses, the same generation-number check that the tool layer applies to superseded workers at the endpoint. Here the fenced party is an observation: a response stamped with an old epoch cannot advance the current continuation, however well formed it is. These control plane identifiers operate alongside, rather than replace, logical operation identifiers and transport attempt counters. A network retry generates a new attempt ID under the same logical operation, whereas an operator interruption bumps the control epoch to invalidate pending replies without erasing the operation’s entry in the operation table. The record now carries several counters, and each advances on different events and rejects a different kind of stale input (table 5).

↩︎ Epoch fencing of superseded workers at the mutating resource is established in Operation identity and endpoint contracts.

Listing 1 expresses these rules as a single supervisory procedure. The legal edges match table 3, and any edge without a registered guard predicate is rejected by default. The caller provides the record_version it observed when formulating its transition request; a concurrent modification by another worker causes the version check to fail. Similarly, duplicate deliveries of an event fail either the version check or the edge guard, guaranteeing that no action is admitted twice.

Here, the guard registered on the READY → CALLING_MODEL edge executes the complete admission check, while admit performs the liability reservations and bookkeeping required by the register-before-send rule. Because the monitor lock protects only in-memory lookups, state updates, and an array append, lock contention remains negligible compared to the hundreds of milliseconds consumed by remote inference or tool execution.

10.4.2 Late results and observation eligibility

When an asynchronous tool result arrives, the harness must reconcile financial and physical state without corrupting the prompt history of an active turn. Upon reception, the runtime authenticates the payload and correlates its task identifier, logical operation ID, and attempt number against the trajectory record. Mismatched or unauthenticated messages are logged for audit and dropped. Valid results immediately update the trajectory’s operation table: the runtime records raw execution evidence and converts reserved charges into settled resource usage.

However, updating the operation table does not imply that the result belongs in the model’s current prompt context. As listing 2 shows, the runtime splits incoming evidence into two separate paths: settlement in the operation table, which every authentic result receives, and epoch-fenced observation staging. Every authentic result records evidence and settles any confirmed usage. An observation, however, is staged for immediate prompt injection only if its call ID remains registered in observation_eligible_calls and the epoch recorded with the call at registration matches the active control_epoch. The remote service never sees the epoch; when the harness correlates the result, it looks up the epoch by call ID and attaches it to the local result envelope as result.epoch. This field comes from the registration record, not the remote payload. The two checks fail in different ways. The eligibility set is the per-call statement of which results the current wait expects, and removing an entry after staging is what stops a redelivery from being staged twice. The epoch is a bulk fence: a timeout or operator interruption advances it once and thereby invalidates every call registered under the old epoch, including any whose removal from the set was missed. If an operation timed out or an operator intervened, its recorded epoch is obsolete. The harness settles the financial balance but refuses to inject the result into the current turn, which eliminates observation crossing. Should that late evidence prove relevant to future turns, the runtime can re-evaluate and stage it during subsequent guarded context assembly. Staging a result does not by itself end the wait. A declared wait policy establishes the criteria required to return from WAITING_TOOL to READY, and the handler requests that transition only when the policy is satisfied.

Systems typically implement one of four wait policies: all required results arrive, an acceptable partial batch completes, a wait deadline expires, or an operator triggers an explicit withdrawal. The trajectory record distinguishes confirmed evidence from unreturned, unresolved calls. In the candidate r18 repair scenario, a timeout records that the outcome was unknown at the deadline; it cannot justify inferring that the test suite failed. When the test service eventually reports that candidate r18 passed, the operation table records that verified outcome with its exact scope. A timeout likewise never authorizes an uncoordinated re-execution of a non-idempotent operation. Dependent actions must still satisfy all guard preconditions and adhere to the tool’s mutation safety contract.

10.4.3 Pause entry and outstanding work

When a pause command arrives while a trajectory is in CALLING_MODEL or WAITING_TOOL, jumping straight into PAUSED would either orphan the in-flight model stream and remote tool job or leave their state transitions ambiguous. The state machine therefore permits entry into PAUSED exclusively from READY. If a pause command arrives mid-call or mid-wait, the runtime asserts a pending_control hold. The hold blocks the admission of any subsequent work, revokes observation eligibility for active model streams, invalidates any pending approval requests, and ends the active observation wait. For a trajectory in WAITING_TOOL, this is the explicit-withdrawal wait policy, and it is what permits the WAITING_TOOL → READY step. If policy dictates, the control plane issues cancellation requests to remote workers; under the settlement rule, these operations remain registered in the operation table until remote confirmation arrives. Any partial token stream received from a canceled model call is preserved solely for diagnostic logging, never stitched together to form a valid dispatch.

Once these local eligibility revocations are logged, the trajectory steps through READY directly into PAUSED under the existing admission hold. Because the lock prevents concurrent scheduling during this step-down, no runnable window opens between the transitions. Entering PAUSED halts continuation progress, ensuring that neither pending responses nor held proposals can advance the continuation. However, background tools already running in external systems may continue their physical execution. For example, the remote test execution for candidate r18 can complete while the trajectory remains paused; its completion payload updates the operation table and settles the reserved charge without triggering a new inference cycle.

Resuming execution requires a guarded PAUSED → READY transition, followed by a brand-new admission check that reconciles all evidence recorded in the operation table during the pause. Resumption is not an automated rollback of pause-time revocations, so state withdrawn during a pause is never restored. Expired authorization tokens remain expired, withdrawn approvals require fresh operator authorization, and canceled partial responses stay abandoned. Any earlier operation that remains unconfirmed continues to block its dependent actions, even if unrelated steps become eligible. Meanwhile, physical resource retention operates under independent lifecycles: sandboxed workspace leases, buffer allocations, compute reservations, and model context caches persist or expire according to their respective lifetime policies rather than the trajectory’s instantaneous state.

10.4.4 Finalization and remaining duties

Ending a run the instant the model claims completion, an operator cancels, or the budget runs out would skip independent acceptance checks, leave delegated credentials active, and abandon unsettled background jobs. Every exit from the live states therefore passes through FINALIZING before reaching a terminal state (figure 2). Upon entering this state, the runtime immediately suspends standard work admission, records the exit reason, revokes unused delegation authority where supported, and triggers the task completion check when completion is claimed. An end-of-turn token emitted by a model merely requests this validation; genuine task completion requires an independent completion check, such as the task contract’s acceptance tests, run outside the model. All finalization checks and mandatory cleanup routines draw from their dedicated, pre-funded reserve.

During finalization, the control plane may block to await pending results, report verification failures, or hand off outstanding cancellation, billing reconciliation, and resource reclamation tasks to an external supervisor. The transition guard into any terminal state requires that every outstanding obligation be either definitively settled or explicitly transferred to an accepting upstream owner. Clearing an unresolved task from a local tracking set violates the guard. To effect a legitimate transfer, the runtime must transmit complete metadata (operation IDs, recorded evidence, and budget reservations), allowing the recipient service to assume supervisory responsibility.

All terminal states (COMPLETED, FAILED, and CANCELED) are strictly absorbing. Once a trajectory enters a terminal state, its continuation terminates permanently. Late-arriving tool results or charge settlements can still update archived historical records through the designated supervisory owner, but no event can transition the continuation back to READY. Resuming an investigation or re-attempting a task requires instantiating a fresh trajectory with a new identifier linked back to the prior run. Within this architecture, COMPLETED denotes solely that the programmatic completion criteria configured for the harness were met. Whether those criteria demonstrate true task competence is an empirical systems question addressed in The Evaluation Problem.

Checkpoint 10.1: Trajectory record and guarded states

Guarded transitions keep every update to the record causally consistent under network delays, tool timeouts, and concurrent interventions. They describe pause entry, however, only from the record’s side. An operator who presses pause needs more: an authenticated way to register the request, a bound on how long it takes effect while a model call is still generating, and a distinction between a pause, a cancellation, and a kill that leaves outstanding work owned in each case.

10.5 Control Actions

The test job for candidate r18 is still executing when an operator requests a pause. The harness must halt the repair loop’s forward progress without losing the job’s tracking identity, its allocated budget reservation, or the diagnostic evidence it might return. Delaying the pause until the test finishes strips the operator of interactive control over potentially runaway execution. Conversely, declaring the job canceled merely because the trajectory state transitions to PAUSED fabricates an execution outcome the runtime has not yet observed.

External control decouples receiving a request from establishing the physical execution state that request demands. When an operator intervenes, the harness authenticates the caller, verifies authority over the target trajectory, and appends the requested directive to pending_control while holding the record lock. Because this update occurs under the lock, the admission-ordering invariants established in section 10.4 fix exactly which operations fall under the request’s scope. The controller acknowledges the hold to the operator only after the update commits to the record (and, once durable execution is in place, to its log). The response delivered to the operator must also distinguish three distinct milestones: request received, new admission stopped, and subsequent confirmation that running work has terminated.

10.5.1 Admission boundaries and active generation

When an operator sends a pause or cancel signal while an agent is preparing a model call or stepping through a batch of proposed tool calls, the harness needs well-defined points where it can stop new work before a request leaves the host. Each such checkpoint is a turn boundary, evaluated immediately before dispatching an inference call to a foundation model and immediately before invoking any external tool, including each individually admitted call within a proposed parallel batch. The name marks where control takes effect, not a boundary between turns: one turn (one step of the trajectory, counted by \(\TurnHorizon\)) contains a turn boundary before its model call and one before each tool call it admits. A pause that lands midway through a batch therefore leaves the calls already sent registered as owned work and withholds the rest. The admission check evaluates control flags under the trajectory record lock, and the release gate serializes its final decision with subsequent control updates. A flag check followed by an uncoordinated send would leave a race window and would not enforce this boundary. The guarantee is local. The boundary fixes which operations were admitted before the request took effect, rather than implying that all work stopped when the operator pressed stop, and it leaves every remaining obligation explicit and inspectable.

Local candidate processing does not have to wait for remote model generation to finish. The foundation-model contract already separates a CANCELED reply from a REJECTED one and withholds both from dispatch; the harness turns that cancellation into a control action. It can initiate streaming cancellation, commanding the serving engine to stop generating an in-progress reply, terminating the invocation’s eligibility to produce an actionable candidate, and retaining partial output solely for bounded diagnosis. A canceled or truncated payload never becomes a dispatchable call, even if a tolerant JSON parser closes its dangling brackets, a valid tool-call prefix appeared before the abort signal, or an apparently complete response arrives after eligibility was withdrawn. The quarantining rule (The normalized status envelope), an application of invariant closure (principle 1) to the reply status, holds regardless of how the invocation ended. Late-arriving evidence remains useful only to reconcile accounting usage or explain what the serving engine executed.

↩︎ The distinction between rejected and canceled streamed replies, and the rule that a partial payload never reaches dispatch, are established in Streaming and cancellation.

3 Why Batching Does Not Shorten the Wait: The reference rate charges each generated token one full read of the weights, the single-request case. A serving engine that batches many requests shares that read among them, which raises throughput but still advances each request by one token per ordinary decode step, so batching cannot bring the per-request wait below this estimate. Techniques that emit several tokens per step change the rate, not the wait’s linear growth with the output allowance.

Output allowances and wall-clock deadlines address different operational risks. An output token ceiling enforces a hard boundary on generated volume under the serving engine’s accounting contract; wall-clock latency, however, also depends on scheduling queues, network stalls, and autoregressive decode throughput. Even under ideal conditions, waiting for a full generation pass introduces substantial delay.3 At a fixed per-token decode time, a call admitted with output allowance \(\OutputLimit\), generating at \(\PerTokenDecodeTime\), can postpone the next turn boundary by approximately

\[\TurnBoundaryDelay \approx \OutputLimit \times \PerTokenDecodeTime\]

Assuming an optimistic decode latency of 21.075 ms per generated token, the single-accelerator, single-request floor of the estimate in Estimating per-call hardware work, with no queueing or memory-access stalls, a 4,096-token allowance postpones the boundary by roughly 86.3 s. Pause or cancellation logic that passively waits for generation to complete would therefore block for more than a minute, and real serving queues only lengthen the wait. A node that splits the weights across several accelerators lowers the per-token floor but leaves the generation estimate linear in \(\OutputLimit\). Halving the allowance therefore halves the full-allowance generation time at a fixed rate, whereas streaming cancellation removes the dependence on generation length entirely. The harness uses both: turn boundaries govern discrete operation admission, while streaming cancellation stops active generations in flight.

Stopping local execution differs from terminating remote execution. A host deadline determines when the supervisor ceases waiting and initiates intervention; confirming that remote inference has actually stopped requires positive evidence from the service. Under the settlement rule, unconfirmed resource usage remains an outstanding liability rather than an assumed refund.

10.5.2 Pause and resume policy

When an operator pauses the repair trajectory while the r18 test container is already running, stepping the record from READY into PAUSED (section 10.4) stops new admissions but leaves a policy choice over the in-flight job itself. One pause policy permits the active r18 test to finish under its existing budget limits while holding the repair loop’s continuation; another requests immediate cancellation and records whatever outcome the executor confirms. A pause policy that permits execution to proceed leaves the workspace live and mutable, and the operator interface must reflect this active state. Any administrative inspection that demands quiescence requires positive confirmation that all concurrent writers have stopped, or else an isolated snapshot taken at a declared boundary. When the operator resumes the trajectory, execution proceeds along the guarded transitions defined in section 10.4; the pause policy governs only what ran while held, without altering the invariants enforced after resumption. Table 6 compares these requested changes to continuation.

Mid-trajectory user steering demands the same transactional discipline. If an operator injects a steering instruction, such as directing the agent to modify a different target file, the harness cannot append the prompt to the conversation context and assume existing operations adapt. Adding instructions does not retroactively invalidate an already admitted file edit or revoke an active tool approval. Instead, the harness records the instruction, determines which plan steps and proposed actions are superseded, and verifies whether outstanding operations conflict with the revised task.

10.5.3 Termination and remaining obligations

When a runaway child process or wedged tool handler ignores a cooperative cancellation request, waiting for polite shutdown leaves the trajectory holding local compute and credentials indefinitely. A kill request therefore invokes immediate, non-cooperative enforcement. Trajectory-level encapsulation (principle 10) is what makes this enforcement possible: because control requests are recorded independently of model output, the runtime can stop new admission and abort the local work it supervises without waiting on the model or on an external service’s response. When a kill signal arrives, the controller halts local execution, revokes mediated access tokens, and fences off obsolete workers across the execution paths under its control. The sandbox reset contract in The reset contract determines which child processes, descendant namespaces, network channels, and allocated resources that enforcement covers. Asynchronous remote work, third-party transactions, and external handles lie beyond direct local process control and demand their own corroborating evidence.

Finalization therefore records confirmed termination separately from unresolved side effects and pending charges, which retain their designated owner under the settlement rule. The operation’s repeat contract determines whether another attempt is safe, and settlement must precede any retry (principle 8); an operation identifier alone cannot reveal what the interrupted execution actually modified.

Enforcing external control decouples trajectory continuation from model behavior and frees execution resources. Worker threads and compute slots never remain blocked while an external job or cancellation response is outstanding. By persisting these control directives and outstanding obligations within the trajectory record, the harness safely suspends execution and yields worker resources, admitting continuation only when verified events satisfy the state machine’s guards.

10.6 Supervised Waiting

When the agent dispatches the integration test for candidate r18, the model generates nothing for as long as the test runs, which is tens of seconds for the integration test priced in Residence time and service rate, and minutes for a broader suite. If the worker thread that dispatched the job blocks until the result returns, it cannot service any other trajectory in the interim, and the runtime becomes unresponsive to operator pauses or high-priority interactive requests. The repair trajectory requires no token generation while the test executes. What it requires instead is an external supervisor: a control entity that tracks the in-flight job, holds the execution continuation, and buffers incoming evidence without tying up a worker thread in a synchronous wait.

Yielding execution through an asynchronous wait4 releases the worker thread immediately, leaving the trajectory record as the explicit holder of pending state. Alternatively, a runtime with blocking workers can maintain controllability if an independent supervisor thread handles operator requests, heartbeats, and deadlines. The requirement holds for any event-loop implementation. Waiting must keep the control record accessible, and any returning event must pass through that record’s admission guards before advancing the trajectory.

4 Event-Driven Waiting: Internet servers faced the thread-per-request version of this problem when a request could wait far longer than it computed. SEDA (Welsh et al. 2001) split services into event-driven stages joined by explicit queues and applied admission control at each queue, so load was visible and bounded where work waited rather than hidden in blocked threads. A harness whose tool jobs run for minutes while a model call takes seconds has the same shape, and the trajectory record is its queue entry.

Welsh, Matt, David Culler, and Eric Brewer. 2001. “SEDA: An Architecture for Well-Conditioned, Scalable Internet Services.” Proceedings of the Eighteenth ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP), 230–43. https://doi.org/10.1145/502034.502057.

10.6.1 The result wakeup pipeline

The asynchronous job interface decouples job acceptance from completion, so a result can arrive seconds or hours after dispatch, out of order, or more than once. The register-before-send rule makes that safe. For an external job, registration records the owning trajectory, the invocation and attempt identifiers, the external job handle when available, the expected workspace source revision, the observation eligibility policy, and the outstanding liability, so even an immediate callback finds the record it needs.

↩︎ The asynchronous tool invocation interface and completion polling contracts are established in Asynchronous Tool Dispatch.

Regardless of how the runtime structures concurrency, the supervisor must satisfy four distinct logical obligations when processing an incoming result. Table 7 stages them in order and pairs each with the inference it does not license.

The event handler generalizes the callback logic in listing 2 across all event sources. Operating under a mutex or transactional boundary, the handler updates the record, advances record_version, and checks the call’s recorded epoch against control_epoch; the version check itself happens in the guarded transition the handler then requests. Because network notifications can be duplicated, the handler must remain strictly idempotent: an identical redelivery will neither append a redundant observation, nor double-bill resource counters, nor re-enqueue an already scheduled continuation. Correlation ensures that incoming data routes to the correct trajectory record, while the tool’s formal contract defines what that evidence actually establishes.

This discipline applies equally to streamed outputs. Real-time log fragments and progress heartbeats can append data to a bounded observation store without terminating the wait interval. Conversely, observing an opening tool-call delimiter in a streaming model response represents an incomplete proposal; the supervisor takes no action until the stream completes. The model adapter retains sole responsibility for parsing and validating full invocation payloads, whereas the harness determines whether that validated proposal remains eligible under current control policies.

10.6.2 Job and batch waits

If a runtime woke the model on every status poll to ask whether to keep waiting, or treated a prompt in raw job stdout as a trigger to resume generation, a ten-minute test run would burn tokens on empty polling turns and let unverified log output drive control decisions. Instead, the harness waits for a push notification or polls external status at bounded intervals on its own, without querying the model. If a job pauses to request human or auxiliary input, the runtime logs that requirement as a structured state transition rather than a trigger for resumption. Responding to such an input requirement remains a distinctly authorized operation, and the record tracks this pending dependency explicitly rather than treating raw stdout or log lines as an implicit signal to resume model generation.

Parallel tool invocations demand an explicit continuation policy. Under an all-results policy, the harness suspends the trajectory until every member of the batch either returns valid evidence or reaches a terminal disposition, such as a timeout with unresolved side effects. Conversely, a partial-results policy permits the model to reason incrementally as individual results arrive, even while other calls remain in flight. Such a policy must explicitly track missing evidence, preventing downstream steps from proceeding under the mistaken assumption that the entire batch has converged.

For example, a completed lint result need not settle a concurrent test job. If the agent’s next planning step requires both checks on candidate r18, the lint output alone leaves the wait open. If, however, the subsequent step is an independent inspection, a partial-results policy can safely admit it under current limits. Modifying the candidate workspace while the test suite actively reads disk state introduces race conditions; simply designating invocations as parallel does not preserve the tested revision without explicit workspace isolation.

When a deadline expires, it terminates the harness’s wait interval, not the underlying remote operation. The harness logs the timeout, marks the operation as unresolved, and requests the configured intervention policy. The operation table keeps the unresolved charge open until explicit settlement rules determine whether late-arriving results can be accepted or whether re-execution is safe.

10.6.3 Results during a pause

If suspending a trajectory also shut down its event listener, any tool callback arriving while the run sat in PAUSED or FINALIZING would be dropped at the socket, stranding the job’s budget reservation and losing its completion evidence. The harness event handler must therefore remain active even when continuation is suspended. When the r18 test result arrives during a pause, it settles its entry in the operation table while the trajectory stays PAUSED along the path traced in section 10.4.3, and only the resume guard can let it inform the next model call. The same holds during finalization, where late-arriving evidence can settle outstanding liabilities without reviving an already terminated trajectory.

10.6.4 Resources retained while waiting

Releasing a worker thread does not dismantle the underlying execution environment: sandboxed processes, disk workspaces, network sockets, buffered logs, and retained inference states remain allocated. The control record maintains explicit references to these resources and their respective lifecycles. Tool subscriptions and cloud storage incur ongoing charges, quota reservations persist until liabilities are settled, and the wall-clock task deadline continues to elapse. Even polling routines and cleanup handlers consume execution budget and must operate within configured limits.

A workspace lease guarantees the availability of candidate source edits and test artifacts according to the lifetime contracts established in Workspace leases, persisting across multiple tool boundaries. Its term bounds what a waiting trajectory can lose. If the harness never returns to renew, lease expiry reclaims the workspace; if it returns after expiry, the resumed trajectory must treat its workspace as gone rather than assume the files it left are still there. In parallel, the model serving infrastructure decides whether to retain, evict, or page out key-value cache state across turns. Releasing the harness worker does not remove the residency tax of Residence time and service rate: attention state kept resident through the wait still divides the turn rate its memory can sustain by \(1 + \ToolTime/\ModelTime\). The harness provides hints regarding expected wait durations and resumption priorities; where a hosted inference service provides no explicit cache-placement primitives, retention remains subject to provider eviction policies.

↩︎ Keep, move, and drop policies for a paused trajectory’s KV-cache state are established in Keep, move, and drop policies.

A wait is therefore a supervised interval characterized by retained evidence, active resource leases, and pending obligations. When that interval concludes, the harness transitions the continuation to an eligible state only if the record’s guards explicitly approve. Human-in-the-loop approval operates under this identical supervisory discipline: rather than waiting on an external computation, the harness suspends execution before dispatch, waiting for an authorization decision that may arrive late, be denied, or expire entirely.

10.7 Approval Gates

Consider the internal-review workflow: an agent proposes submitting candidate patch r18 along with selected verification records, but trusted policy permits submission only after an authorized engineer signs off. The proposal is complete, yet the human reviewer is unavailable. Meanwhile, the task’s deadline clock continues advancing, an operator can request a pause, and credentials or access grants can expire. The harness cannot simply leave the proposal floating in the model’s context window, nor can it allow a retry loop or subsequent model step to dispatch the action prematurely. Instead, the runtime must hold the fully formed submission in escrow while review remains unresolved.

↩︎ Action-bound approval, which ties sign-off on the r18 patch submission to its exact canonical payload, is established in Action-bound approval.

Definition 10.3: Approval escrow

Approval escrow is the harness’s retention of an undispatched proposal pending required approval and subsequent dispatch admission. The held record binds the proposal to its enclosing task, approval conditions, and expiration deadline; even after a favorable decision arrives, execution still requires current admission checks at the dispatch boundary.

  1. Significance: Escrow decouples proposing an operation from the authority to execute it immediately, keeping long, human-scale waits visible to supervisors and external schedulers.
  2. Distinction: Authorization policies define who holds the authority to approve a represented action. The harness manages the operational lifecycle of that pending decision: routing the request, tracking timeouts, and enforcing state transitions toward dispatch or refusal.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a recorded approval as an unconditional instruction to execute, ignoring subsequent changes to credentials, task deadlines, branch states, or available compute capacity.

Requiring human approval is a deliberate task-policy choice rather than an automatic property of irreversible side effects. Some high-risk actions are prohibited outright; many others proceed safely under tight, automated least-privilege policies. Conversely, sensitive data disclosures or even benign, reversible mutations might require human oversight depending on operational risk. The approval gate must therefore enforce trusted runtime policy over the concrete operation, target resource, and parameter scope, never relying on the model’s self-reported assessment of whether an action is safe. Requesting review cannot expand authority: a reviewer cannot grant permissions beyond their own administrative boundaries.

When a safety policy such as a production code freeze exists solely as prompt instructions inside the model’s context window, no gate stands between a proposed mutation and the target system (war story 10.1).

War Story 10.1: A code freeze stated only to the model (2025)
Context: In July 2025, SaaStr founder Jason Lemkin was building an application with Replit’s autonomous coding agent and placed the project under an explicit code freeze, instructing the agent in natural language not to modify code or production infrastructure (Sharwood 2025).

Mechanism: The freeze existed only as text in the agent’s conversational context. No supervisory component at the dispatch boundary validated proposed database tool calls against that restriction. Despite the instructions, the agent executed an operation that deleted the production database, subsequently claiming that rollback was impossible.

Impact: The live production database was unavailable until engineering restored it. Lemkin also reported that the agent had masked previous software faults with fabricated synthetic data, including populating a 4,000-record database with fictional user profiles.

Fix: Contrary to the agent’s assertion, database snapshot rollback functioned properly and restored the data. Lemkin observed that natural language instructions cannot reliably enforce operational boundaries in agentic coding environments, advocating for strict architectural isolation between preview, staging, and production environments.

Systems lesson: Operational freezes are admission-control invariants, and admission control belongs strictly to the execution runtime. Stating a freeze to the model merely shifts output probabilities; it provides no deterministic safety guarantees. When enforced at the dispatch boundary by checking the target endpoint and operation payload against active policy, the runtime deterministically traps or rejects forbidden mutations regardless of what the model generates. This reflects the foundational requirement of invariant closure (principle 1).

From the perspective of harness architecture, enforcing the freeze requires a single dispatch-time rule: while the freeze flag is set, any proposed mutation against the production target must be rejected or held in approval escrow. A destructive action then collapses into a harmless refusal observation fed back to the model rather than an unrecoverable side effect. Whether the model follows instructions becomes irrelevant to system correctness. The agent’s false claim that rollback was impossible highlights a related boundary: a model-generated status report can be false. The harness must verify execution outcomes and rollback states directly from its operation table, never from the agent’s conversational transcript.

Approval gates must also remain selective and narrow. When a human operator faces dozens of routine, low-risk confirmation dialogs every hour, fatigue leads to rubber-stamping without inspection.5 A gate where every request is approved adds no closure beyond the policy check that routed the request to it. Fine-grained authorization grants, sandboxes, and automated linters should handle the vast majority of verifiable actions. Reserving manual approval for high-blast-radius or policy-mandated exceptions keeps human attention on the decisions that need it.

5 Ironies of Automation: Bainbridge observed that automating most of a process leaves the human operator the residual job of monitoring it, and that people sustain attention poorly on a stream in which almost nothing goes wrong (Bainbridge 1983). An approval queue in which nearly every request is benign is that stream. The one harmful proposal arrives in a queue that has trained the reviewer to expect nothing, which is why the gate’s precision, not its presence, determines what closure it supplies.

Bainbridge, Lisanne. 1983. “Ironies of Automation.” Automatica 19 (6): 775–79. https://doi.org/10.1016/0005-1098(83)90046-8.

10.7.1 Held proposals

If an approval request pointed only to a mutable workspace path or an unversioned tool call, a concurrent edit or plan revision could change the underlying patch between the moment the reviewer inspected r18 and the moment the harness dispatched the submission. After validating a completed model response, the harness therefore freezes the held proposal in the trajectory record before publishing its approval request. The record references the task and requester, intended tool origin and operation, immutable candidate artifact and selected evidence, applicable preconditions, required approver policy, and approval expiry. It also assigns an approval-request identifier and a held-proposal version, and computes the approval digest, the digest that action-bound approval binds, computed here over the canonical held proposal and covering the operation, its arguments, its target, and the versions of the referenced artifacts. The approval binds to that digest, so changing any argument or artifact produces a different digest and voids the approval. These identifiers and the cryptographic digest track the pending decision itself, distinct from the logical operation IDs or transport-layer idempotency tokens assigned once dispatch begins.

For the review submission, the held artifact is the exact r18 patch and named check records, and the selected records retain their evidential scope; a targeted unit-test pass does not become full acceptance because it appears in an approval request. The authentication and represented-action requirements are those of action-bound approval. The harness adds the recorded status needed to distinguish pending, denied, expired, withdrawn, and admitted requests. In figure 3, panel (a) binds the exact proposal payload, artifact hashes, destination scope, and expiration timestamp into an immutable approval digest for revision r18. Panel (b) traces the four-step resolution sequence that parks the trajectory in AWAITING_APPROVAL until reviewer decisions and dispatch guards are verified.

The trajectory enters AWAITING_APPROVAL without sending the held tool call. Parking the trajectory in this state releases its active compute worker; polling for a human response requires an external callback or light notification check rather than expensive model inference. The suspended trajectory retains its proposal record, cryptographic digest, and artifact references, while optionally maintaining its workspace lease and sandbox storage.

Under stable flow, Little’s law (theorem 5.1) makes the mean number of trajectories held in AWAITING_APPROVAL equal to the rate of approval requests multiplied by the mean time to a decision, \(\ApprovalHeldTrajectories = \ArrivalRate\,\ExternalWait\). Review latency, not model speed, dictates how many concurrent held trajectories and retained resources the system carries. The relation fixes the mean, not the peak. A burst of review requests after an incident, or a reviewer who clears the queue once a day, holds far more than the mean for as long as the backlog lasts. The pool for held workspaces is therefore sized from the arrival pattern. The approval expiry strictly bounds this wait for a decision, while the trajectory’s overall deadline clock advances independently. Which operational cost dominates, retained sandbox footprint or delayed mitigation, depends on the workload profile.

↩︎ Little’s law, which equates mean occupancy with arrival rate times mean residence time, is established in Residence time and service rate.

Lighthouse 10.1: Remediation agent (approval escrow)
Workload: The remediation agent diagnoses a production incident and proposes a change, such as restoring a previous service configuration, that policy holds for an on-call reviewer. The repair agent’s gated action is instead a review submission near the end of a long trajectory. Both wait in AWAITING_APPROVAL, and Little’s law, \(\ApprovalHeldTrajectories = \ArrivalRate\,\ExternalWait\) for the \(\ApprovalHeldTrajectories\) trajectories held at once over a decision time \(\ExternalWait\), prices each wait. All rates and times below are scenario inputs.

Repair agent: Review requests arrive at 6 per hour and a decision takes 2 hours on average, so about 12 trajectories are held at once. Each keeps its workspace lease, 512 MiB in the reference coding-agent profile, so escrow retains 6 GiB of sandbox memory while no user waits on the outcome.

Remediation agent: Requests arrive at 2 per hour, so on average 0.5 trajectories are held and retained resources are negligible. The cost is time: with 6 minutes of diagnosis, a 15-minute mean decision, and 9 minutes to apply and verify, approval is 50 percent of the 30-minute time to mitigate, all of it spent with the service degraded.

Systems insight: One escrow mechanism binds on different terms. For the repair agent, retained memory scales with \(\ExternalWait\), so halving the decision time halves the held workspace. For the remediation agent, each minute removed from \(\ExternalWait\) removes a minute of degradation, and approval is the largest term in time to mitigate. Remediation therefore binds the approval digest to one exact configuration version so review is quick, sets the approval expiry shorter than the interval over which production configuration drifts, and lets already-admitted independent read-only diagnostic jobs continue under their existing grants and limits while the mutation waits.

10.7.2 Approval decisions and duplicate delivery

Webhook deliveries from an approval service can arrive twice after a network retry, or arrive late after the request has already expired or been withdrawn by an operator. Rather than blindly treating any inbound approval packet as an immediate trigger to resume execution, the harness requires an authenticated decision cryptographically tied to the held request identifier and version. It validates the decision against the active escrow record and recalculates the approval digest. Duplicate webhooks or replayed messages are idempotent; the harness drops repeated deliveries without recording duplicate decisions or re-triggering dispatch. Similarly, a late decision arriving for a request that has already been withdrawn, expired, or superseded serves only as historical audit evidence; it can never revive a closed request. Table 8 distinguishes recording such a decision from admitting the operation.

Silence leaves the request unapproved. When the approval timeout lapses, the runtime logs an expiration event rather than an active human rejection. Both outcomes prevent dispatch, but the distinction matters when the trajectory is later debugged or audited. If the agent’s task policy permits continuation after a refusal, the harness delivers a compact observation record confirming that the operation was withheld:

{"approval_id": "review_r18",
 "status": "REFUSED", "reason": "APPROVAL_EXPIRED",
 "dispatch_status": "not_sent"}

The model can then interpret this refusal observation and propose an alternative strategy. What it cannot do is unilaterally extend the expired approval by submitting identical requests in a tight loop. Generating a fresh approval request requires constructing a current proposal that undergoes a new policy evaluation; otherwise, an automated agent could flood the reviewer with repeated requests, a denial of service through approval fatigue. If the enclosing task has exhausted its wall-clock budget, the harness records the refusal directly in the trajectory’s final record without expending further tokens or compute to solicit a narrative explanation from the model.

10.7.3 Dispatch-time rechecks

Between the moment a reviewer signs off on the r18 submission and the moment a worker prepares to dispatch it, the target branch may have advanced, the task’s token or financial budget may have been exhausted, or short-lived credentials may have lapsed.
