Human approval therefore satisfies only the governance precondition for admission, never exempting the held proposal from standard admission control when dispatch begins.

At the dispatch boundary, the harness re-evaluates the entire operational state. It recomputes the approval digest to ensure the artifact has not been modified in place, checks that the approval lease has not expired, and executes the full suite of rate-limit, budget, and authorization checks. Because queueing delays and downstream rate limits can stall an approved request in escrow, checks validated when the approval message arrived cannot be grandfathered into execution. If any check fails, the harness logs the specific refusal reason; if a task-level termination triggers, the trajectory routes immediately to finalization.

Consider a submission that requires an unmodified target branch. Reading the branch HEAD before requesting approval cannot prevent concurrent commits during the minutes or hours the request sits in escrow. When operations carry concurrency preconditions, the dispatching endpoint must verify the expected target version atomically alongside the mutation, or use an external optimistic locking mechanism.

↩︎ The atomic conditional update, which verifies an expected version in the same step as the write, is established in Conditional updates.

Admitting a held request follows the register-before-send protocol with one additional step in the same atomic update. Under an exclusive lock, the harness registers the outgoing operation and marks the held escrow record as consumed. This prevents duplicate approval callbacks or concurrent workers from dispatching the action twice, and it strictly orders an approver’s sign-off against a racing operator cancellation or timeout. If a cancellation commits first, it withdraws the held request and records an unambiguous not_sent status. If admission commits first, the operation becomes owned work. A later control may still withhold release through the serialized gate, but the harness retains the record and reservation until non-release and the absence of remote liability are established. If an unhandled transport failure occurs after the packet departs the host, the harness must treat the remote effect as indeterminate. While marking an approval consumed prevents duplicate local dispatch, it cannot guarantee endpoint deduplication or resolve an in-flight network partition.

10.7.4 Withdrawal of held requests

If an operator pauses a trajectory while a proposal sits in AWAITING_APPROVAL, leaving the request active in the review queue would let a reviewer’s click trigger dispatch while the trajectory is supposed to be held. Pausing from AWAITING_APPROVAL therefore follows the supervisory lifecycle in section 10.4 with an explicit escrow cleanup step: the harness immediately withdraws the pending approval request, marks the proposal as not dispatched in the audit trail, and transitions through READY to PAUSED. If an approver submits a late decision while the task is paused or after it resumes, the harness rejects the decision because its associated request was already revoked. Should the agent still require the operation upon resuming, it must generate a fresh proposal subject to current state and policy checks.

Cancellation and task expiration route directly into FINALIZING. This terminal path requires all previously dispatched work and outstanding external resources to be settled or transferred to an accepting owner. Withdrawing a held review submission leaves no side effects on external targets, but it does not automatically terminate concurrent asynchronous test jobs, settle their compute costs, or undo previously committed mutations. By maintaining a strict separation between held proposals in escrow and committed entries in the operation table, the harness provides unambiguous operational visibility even when complex trajectories terminate abruptly.

Checkpoint 10.2: Approval over time

The internal-review submission for r18 is held while the task approaches its deadline.

The approval gate now has a bounded lifetime and an explicit handoff from held proposal to admitted work. Human agreement is one condition in that handoff, alongside current authority, resource bounds, and action-time preconditions. Multiple trajectories can satisfy those conditions while competing for the same execution capacity, so the harness next needs a policy for sharing that capacity without bypassing their individual gates.

10.8 Scheduling Concurrent Trajectories

When the test suite for candidate r18 finishes and the repair trajectory returns to READY, it rarely has the serving cluster to itself: an interactive user session and a backlog of repository maintenance tasks may already be waiting to query the model. If the harness simply dispatches requests as soon as tasks generate them, aggressive retry loops will monopolize model capacity; if it unconditionally favors interactive requests, large maintenance jobs can wait indefinitely. Concurrency alone does not solve resource allocation, so the harness needs an explicit scheduling policy to arbitrate among eligible calls.

The unit of scheduling admission is a READY trajectory’s next model call, rather than its entire multi-step task or the downstream tool execution that the reply may propose. Completing an asynchronous wait does not grant immediate dispatch; it makes a trajectory eligible only through a guarded transition into the READY state, where it queues for the scheduler’s approval. Downstream, the inference serving engine manages batching, KV-cache allocation, and accelerator execution. That low-level engine scheduling remains strictly decoupled from the harness’s control plane, which decides which high-level trajectory may submit work in the first place.

10.8.1 Fair sharing and the scheduling charge

A single maintenance prompt containing extensive code diffs can consume 40,000 tokens while a concise interactive query requires only 2,000 tokens, so granting an equal number of model calls across classes does not deliver equal service. A scheduler must therefore define what resource its allocations apportion. The policy used here bills each admitted request a declared scheduling charge equal to its full input-token count plus its maximum allowed output-token reservation. This conservative metric measures admitted token commitments rather than actual generated output, monetary cost, accelerator occupancy, or task utility. Cached prompt tokens remain part of this upfront charge because serving-engine cache hits cannot be guaranteed before dispatch.

Weighted deficit round-robin provides an effective, low-overhead mechanism for apportioning this declared token charge across diverse service classes. For each class, the scheduler keeps a credit counter and a queue of the class’s READY trajectories, served in rotation from a pointer that persists between rounds. At the beginning of each scheduling round, the system increments each class’s credit counter by a quantum proportional to its assigned weight. The scheduler then steps through the classes, admitting calls as long as their token charges fit within the available credit and all downstream capacity gates pass. Each admission deducts the call’s charge from the class balance. Unspent credit carries over into subsequent rounds, allowing a class with large requests to gradually accumulate enough deficit credit to admit a multi-thousand-token prompt. When a class exhausts its active work, the scheduler resets its credit counter to zero so it cannot hoard unearned allocations during idle periods. This design adapts the deficit carryover mechanism6 of deficit round-robin (Shreedhar and Varghese 1995) from network packet scheduling to language model admission control.

6 From Fair Queueing to Deficit Round-Robin: Fair queueing for packet networks (Demers et al. 1989) emulated a bit-by-bit round-robin across flows, ensuring that high-volume sources could not exceed their fair shares, but it required ordering packets by computed finish times. Deficit round-robin reached comparable fairness with constant work per packet by giving each queue a quantum and carrying unspent credit forward (Shreedhar and Varghese 1995). Because model-call charges span orders of magnitude, deficit carryover, rather than simple queue rotation, is what enforces proportional fairness here.

Demers, Alan, Srinivasan Keshav, and Scott Shenker. 1989. “Analysis and Simulation of a Fair Queueing Algorithm.” Proceedings of the Symposium on Communications Architectures and Protocols (SIGCOMM ’89), 1–12. https://doi.org/10.1145/75246.75248.
Shreedhar, M., and George Varghese. 1995. “Efficient Fair Queuing Using Deficit Round Robin.” Proceedings of the Conference on Applications, Technologies, Architectures, and Protocols for Computer Communication (SIGCOMM ’95), 231–42. https://doi.org/10.1145/217382.217453.

Credit belongs to the service class rather than to individual trajectories, so a tenant cannot enlarge its share by spawning hundreds of concurrent tasks. Within a class, the rotation pointer persists across passes; a scan that restarted at the head of the queue on every pass would let trajectories near the front absorb fresh credit while those near the tail starved. Every class expected to make progress needs a positive weight, and any ceiling on accumulated credit must accommodate the largest single request the policy permits. A credit cap below that size leaves the largest calls stalled indefinitely despite repeated replenishment rounds.

Napkin Math 10.1: Charge shares vs. call shares
Problem: An interactive class has weight 3 and a maintenance class has weight 1, with a base quantum of 8,000 charge tokens per unit weight per round. An interactive call carries 2,000 input tokens and a 500-token output allowance; a maintenance call carries 40,000 input tokens and a 2,000-token output allowance. Assume both classes remain continuously backlogged at these fixed call sizes, no provider or local-capacity gate binds, and carried credit can cover the largest permitted call. Over many rounds, how many calls does each class admit?

Math: Each round credits 24,000 tokens to the interactive class and 8,000 tokens to maintenance. Because interactive calls incur a scheduling charge of 2,500 tokens each, the interactive class averages 24,000 / 2,500 = 9.6 calls per round. A maintenance call incurs a charge of 42,000 tokens, so its class must carry forward credit for 42,000 / 8,000 = 5.25 rounds per call.

Result: The interactive class receives 75 percent of the total token charge capacity, but it dispatches roughly 50 times as many admitted calls as the maintenance class.

Systems insight: Class weights determine the long-term division of token charges, while admitted call rates depend directly on request size. The maintenance class’s mean interval between admissions, measured in credit rounds, equals its request charge divided by its per-round quantum under the stated assumptions. Halving its input to 20,000 tokens reduces this aggregate interval to approximately 2.8 rounds per admission without changing class weights. An individual trajectory also waits behind its class peers; execution, tool waits, and approval time lie outside this quotient. Increasing a class weight shifts its aggregate token share; shrinking prompt sizes (through prompt compaction or targeted context retrieval) accelerates its call dispatch rate. Finally, the scheduler’s credit ceiling must accommodate at least one complete maintenance charge, or that class will never accumulate sufficient deficit credit to dispatch.

Weights split the token charge; request size then sets a far larger split in calls.

Configuring class weights fixes how the declared token charge is shared, not the proportion of admitted calls, and it divides capacity in proportion to weight rather than creating capacity. Under sustained overload, scheduling weights redistribute queueing delay without increasing provider throughput, so bounding latency under persistent saturation requires admission limits on incoming tasks or explicit rejection.

10.8.2 Quota and capacity gates

A model call selected by the fairness scheduler can still fail at dispatch with an HTTP 429 rejection if the tenant’s provider quota is full, or stall downstream if local sandboxes are out of memory. Before dispatching a nominated call, the harness must therefore pass two capacity gates: external provider quotas and local execution capacity. Model providers enforce rate limits across distinct dimensions, specifically requests per minute (RPM), tokens per minute (TPM), and concurrent in-flight requests, and the provider’s accounting contract determines what must be reserved before transmission. For token limits, providers typically debit the full input count plus the declared maximum output allowance against the current time window until generation finishes. Pricing discounts do not relax rate limits; a provider that discounts cached prompt tokens on the invoice may still count those tokens at full weight against the tenant’s rolling TPM window. Satisfying a token quota likewise guarantees nothing about request concurrency, which is capped independently to protect serving infrastructure.

A local rate limiter lets the harness hold pending calls rather than discover provider saturation through HTTP 429 (Too Many Requests) rejections. It must account for all traffic that shares the provider allocation, including harness processes on other machines. When that traffic or the provider’s windowing algorithm is opaque, it keeps safety headroom and adjusts its refill rate from response headers that report remaining quota and suggested retry intervals.

Local execution resources impose a second set of gates. A tool call that compiles code or runs an integration test needs sandbox slots, CPU cores, memory, and storage bandwidth even when model token quotas are abundant. Each bottleneck must be tracked in its own physical unit: bytes of disk space, bytes per second of transfer, operations per second of storage I/O. Counting homogeneous sandbox slots suffices only when every job fits an identical envelope; with heterogeneous jobs, slot counts alone do not prevent out-of-memory failures. Tool dispatch therefore has its own admission queue, shared by policy in these physical units rather than in model tokens. A protected action whose approval arrives when no sandbox slot is free stays in AWAITING_APPROVAL alongside its recorded decision (table 8) instead of returning to READY and issuing another model call.

Selection by the scheduler is a nomination, not an execution grant. Between the scheduler picking a candidate and committing its dispatch, an operator can pause the trajectory, a grant can expire, or a concurrent step can consume the remaining budget. The nominated call therefore still passes the atomic admission check, which records every quota and capacity reservation before the request leaves the host. If two scheduler workers nominate the same candidate, the record_version check lets exactly one commit. A call blocked by insufficient credit, an exhausted rate limit, or a full sandbox pool stays in READY; that delay is backpressure, not task failure. Once a call is sent, its reservations follow the settlement rule. An HTTP timeout after transmission keeps them outstanding, because the provider may have processed and billed the request, and provider-quota refunds follow the provider’s own accounting contract.

Fair sharing decides which eligible trajectory is served next, and quota and capacity gates decide whether that work can run now. Neither limits the total work one trajectory requests across many turns; a repair agent can receive its fair share of tokens indefinitely without converging on a valid patch. That limit is the trajectory budget.

10.9 Budgets and Ceilings

A repair trajectory that receives its fair share of model service on every round can still loop across dozens of failing patch attempts without converging, or launch a new model generation when an unbilled test job in flight has already committed the rest of its allowance. Fair scheduling and provider quotas govern how a trajectory shares cluster capacity at a single step, not how much total work one trajectory may accumulate or how much uncommitted liability it carries while background jobs run. Bounding cumulative consumption therefore requires a multi-dimensional trajectory budget enforced at admission against worst-case in-flight liabilities and sized from empirical task outcomes.

10.9.1 Turn, token, time, and dollar budgets

When the repair agent generates a candidate code patch that fails its regression test, the model often responds by proposing a slight variant, followed by another permutation and eventually a broader diagnostic routine that also fails. Each failure appends an error trace to the conversational context while every individual tool call remains syntactically well formed and stays within per-invocation token and compute limits. The trajectory therefore yields cleanly at every tool boundary, receives its scheduled turn on the inference server, and satisfies every local admission check, yet dozens of turns later this runaway retry loop has burned through substantial cloud and inference budgets while making zero measurable progress toward resolving the defect. In figure 4, failing tests append repeated error observations to the context until turn-completion proposals vanish. The same figure shows the external budget that halts the cycle at the admission boundary and moves the trajectory into FINALIZING once the next call’s liability no longer fits the allowance.

Ending such a loop is the harness’s responsibility. As failure outputs accumulate in the prompt context, they dilute the original system instructions and task specifications. Under severe context dilution, model generations that terminate the trajectory become increasingly rare. Prompt-level guardrails, such as injecting notices about remaining funds or requesting that the model self-regulate under a cost ceiling, offer probabilistic nudges rather than deterministic bounds, and their effect weakens as the context fills with repeated stack traces. Because an errant model remains syntactically coherent and locally compliant while failing globally, trajectory bounds must reside within the external supervisory control plane (principle 10). Deciding whether an agent has stalled requires complex semantic heuristics, but an external budget deterministically stops further ordinary admission when the allowance is exhausted.

↩︎ Attention dilution as stale observations and superseded logs accumulate in the working context is established in Testing usable working context.

To constrain total resource consumption, the harness establishes a multi-dimensional per-trajectory budget, the vector

\[\TrajectoryBudget = \langle \TrajectoryTurnLimit,\ \TrajectoryTokenLimit,\ \TrajectoryTimeLimit,\ \CostCeiling \rangle\]

where the components establish formal allowances on execution turns (\(\TrajectoryTurnLimit\)), cumulative tokens (\(\TrajectoryTokenLimit\)), elapsed wall-clock time (\(\TrajectoryTimeLimit\)), and total financial cost (\(\CostCeiling\)). The harness configuration must unambiguously define what increments each meter: admitted model invocations increment the turn counter, effective input prompt tokens plus generated output tokens increment the token counter, and direct API charges, sandboxed execution fees, and paid third-party tool usage increment the dollar counter. Retried operations, network reconnects, and failed invocations consume real resources and must be billed against these totals. Production harnesses frequently extend this vector with additional domain-specific limits, such as sandbox disk allocations or maximum external API query counts.

Each dimension governs a distinct operational constraint. A trajectory with very few turns can rapidly exhaust financial limits through large-context reasoning models, while an inexpensive agent can tie up execution sandboxes across long asynchronous polling delays. Unlike consumable token or dollar allowances, elapsed time represents a monotonically advancing wall-clock deadline measured from task initiation. Network delays, sandboxed compilation phases, human approval holds, and backoff pauses all advance this deadline unless the task contract explicitly provides a paused clock. When the primary execution deadline expires, the harness halts the admission of new exploratory work while supervising in-flight background operations. Post-execution finalization requires its own separately allocated time allowance and reserved funding; an expired exploration deadline must never trigger an unconstrained cleanup phase that runs indefinitely.

The harness may safely expose current budget metrics to the model, enabling the agent to adjust its planning strategy toward cheaper alternatives. However, the model must never possess the authority to modify accounting records, release reservations, or exempt its own calls from admission gates. This separation allows cooperative optimization while guaranteeing that system bounds remain strictly invariant to model behavior.

10.9.2 Reservation and settlement

If the harness checks only settled invoices before admitting a new model call, a trajectory with an unbilled test job in flight and a 4,096-token generation ahead can pass admission and then exceed its dollar ceiling once both bills arrive. A budget serves as a hard ceiling only when the admission gate sets aside the worst-case cost of in-flight and proposed work before authorizing new liabilities.7 The amount set aside for an admitted operation is its budget reservation, the operation’s maximum additional charge, withheld from the allowance at admission and released only when evidence settles the operation. The chapter keeps the word escrow for held proposals awaiting approval, which hold an action rather than an amount. Test-time search reserved each candidate’s maximum completion before dispatch and kept unsettled reservations against its quota; the harness extends that discipline from one search to every operation of the trajectory, including tool jobs and the finalization reserve.

7 Reservation Against an Invariant: Database systems solved a version of this problem for shared quantities such as account balances and inventory counts. O’Neil’s escrow method (where escrow names a set-aside quantity, not the held action of this chapter’s approval escrow) let a transaction set aside part of a quantity only after testing that the quantity’s invariant would hold whichever concurrent transactions later committed or aborted (O’Neil 1986). Bounded-liability admission applies the same test to a trajectory’s allowance, with the operation’s maximum possible charge standing in for an exact decrement.

O’Neil, Patrick E. 1986. “The Escrow Transactional Method.” ACM Transactions on Database Systems 11 (4): 405–30. https://doi.org/10.1145/7239.7265.

↩︎ Reserving a candidate’s maximum completion before dispatch, within a task-wide quota, is established in Reserving budget before admitting work.

Definition 10.4: Bounded-liability admission

Bounded-liability admission is the harness rule that admits a new operation only when, in every cumulative budget dimension, actual consumption plus outstanding reservations plus the operation’s maximum additional charge plus the protected finalization reserve fits within the allowance.

  1. Significance: It turns a budget that is merely reported into an enforceable upper bound on per-attempt expenditure (the numerator of cost per accepted task, equation 1.2) while guaranteeing that essential post-task verification checks remain fully funded.
  2. Distinction: Whereas a simple spend check merely compares settled invoices against the allowance, bounded-liability admission reserves capacity for in-flight requests and charges the worst-case ceiling of the pending operation.
  3. Common pitfall: Prematurely releasing a reservation upon a network timeout or client-side cancellation before upstream providers settle actual usage. This error exposes uncommitted budget that concurrent operations can claim before delayed billing charges arrive.

For each cumulative resource dimension, the admission test is

\[\text{actual consumption} + \text{outstanding reservations} + \text{proposed bounded liability} + \text{protected finalization reserve} \leq \text{allowance} \tag{1}\]

The harness evaluates this inequality componentwise across all cumulative resource dimensions, while evaluating wall-clock deadlines through a separate timing guard. The term for outstanding reservations encompasses all admitted but unsettled operations, including concurrent inference requests and active paid tools. The protected finalization reserve isolates dedicated funding for validation runs, report generation, and environment teardown, preventing exploratory steps from exhausting the funds needed to verify final results. When the trajectory transitions into finalization, the harness reallocates the necessary portion of this protected reserve into active operation reservations, avoiding duplicate accounting. Because the inequality is the budget clause of the admission check, it runs under the record lock with the rest of that check, and the register-before-send rule keeps two concurrent subtasks from reading the same uncommitted headroom and spending it twice.

Listing 3 assembles the pieces introduced so far into one turn: the guarded transition of listing 1, the reservation that admission makes, the status routing of table 4, the approval hold, and register-before-send for tool calls. The reservation is made inside the guarded transition into CALLING_MODEL, so an unaffordable call is refused before any request leaves the host. Usage settles the reservation after the reply, and the reply’s status decides the next state before any content can become a tool call. Admission never depends on a charge discovered after the call.

The reserve step evaluates equation 1 and aborts the state transition whenever the proposed invocation exceeds available headroom; route_status maps terminal inference conditions according to table 4. For clarity, the listing abstracts batched invocation checks and multi-step approval lifecycles. Its invoke_model and dispatch helpers also include the serialized release gate: they recheck the admitted operation against current controls before release, retain ownership and liability if release is withheld, and exit through guarded refusal or control handling without returning a fabricated remote reply. These helpers perform no second reservation and keep slow network I/O outside the record lock. This listing describes live-host supervision; crash reconstruction requires the durable evidence developed in Durable Execution.

A control action can also commit while run_turn is blocked in invoke_model, because that call runs outside the lock. The control path then performs the pause entry itself: it withdraws the call’s eligibility, cancels the stream if policy says so, and steps the record through READY to PAUSED. When invoke_model returns, settle still records the call’s usage, but the next transition starts from PAUSED. Every edge it could request from there fails: the edges to WAITING_TOOL and AWAITING_APPROVAL are not legal from PAUSED, and the guards on the legal ones, to READY and FINALIZING, admit only the control path’s own resume or stop decisions. The transition raises IllegalTransition, and the reply is kept only as diagnostic evidence, as a CANCELED reply would be.

For a model call, the proposed bounded liability prices every input token at the undiscounted input rate \(\InputTokenPrice\), cached or not, and the full output allowance \(\OutputLimit\) at the output rate \(\OutputTokenPrice\):

\[\text{liability} = \InputTokenPrice\, \SequenceLength + \OutputTokenPrice\, \OutputLimit \tag{2}\]

Here \(\SequenceLength\) counts the whole prompt, unlike the uncached-only count used for settled charges in section 10.9.3. Pricing the prompt at full rate keeps the bound valid when an expected cache hit fails to materialize, and charging the whole allowance keeps it valid when the model generates up to its cap; settlement later replaces both worst cases with reported usage.

Even when the unreserved balance remains strictly positive, it may prove insufficient to cover the maximum liability of the next operation. A naive spend check admits such a call on the basis of past settled invoices, only to exceed the budget ceiling once the provider bills the resulting tokens. While shrinking the requested maximum output tokens can lower the call’s worst-case liability, this optimization succeeds only if prompt tokens and outstanding tool charges still fit within the margin. External paid tools that lack enforceable execution timeouts or bounded billing tiers cannot provide a finite liability ceiling either. Under a strict enforcement policy, the harness must either reject such open-ended tools outright, substitute bounded proxies, or explicitly declare an approximate threshold that tolerates financial overshoot. Enforcing a client-side network timeout does not provide an immutable cost ceiling because the remote provider may continue billing until its own worker processes finish.

Napkin Math 10.2: A call the spend check admits
Problem: A repair trajectory has a $3.50 allowance, $2.58 of settled spend, a $0.50 reservation on its unsettled test job, and a $0.30 finalization reserve. The next model call carries 50,000 input tokens and a 4,096-token output allowance, reserved at hypothetical prices of $3 and $15 per million input and output tokens. Should the harness admit it?

Math: The call’s bounded liability is $0.15 of input plus $0.06 of output, $0.21 in all. A spend check sees $3.50 − $2.58 = $0.92 of headroom. Equation 1 subtracts the reservation and the finalization reserve as well, leaving $0.12.

Result: The spend check admits the call; bounded-liability admission refuses it. Admitted, the call would leave the final checks $0.09 short once the test settles in full.

Systems insight: Input is 70.9 percent of this call’s liability, so trimming the output allowance barely helps. The call fits only if its input falls to about 19,500 tokens, through compaction, or if the test job settles and releases part of its reservation. Every $0.10 of unsettled liability removes about 33,300 input tokens from what the next call may carry, which is why settling promptly from usage evidence is worth more than a smaller output cap.

The spend check sees room for the call; after reservations, it no longer fits.

Settlement replaces the reservation with verified consumption once the harness receives an authenticated, correlated usage report from the provider. An incremental report moves confirmed charges into the settled meter while the remaining liability stays reserved, and only terminal billing evidence releases the residual; a dropped stream, missing telemetry, or client-side timeout shows nothing about what the provider billed. Cached input is reserved at the full rate unless the provider contractually guarantees the hit, and it is settled at the cached rate the usage report shows. Canceling a stream early can likewise lower the eventual output charge, but admission of later work cannot count on a cancellation that has not yet settled. The ceiling holds because every request passes the check (complete mediation (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975)), every reservation is a worst-case bound, and only verified execution records settle it.

10.9.3 Metered accounting and prefix cache reuse

Once a model call or tool job finishes, the harness replaces its worst-case reservation with the actual bill, and that bill depends heavily on whether the serving engine reused cached prefix state or had to reprocess the prompt from scratch. Inference providers bill token usage in distinct categories: uncached input tokens (\(\UncachedInputTokenCount\)), cached prompt tokens (\(\CachedInputTokenCount\)), and generated output tokens (\(\OutputLength\)). For any completed turn \(\TurnIndex\), the harness updates the accumulated cost according to:

\[\SettledTrajectoryCost^{(\TurnIndex)} = \SettledTrajectoryCost^{(\TurnIndex-1)} + \InputTokenPrice\, \UncachedInputTokenCount^{(\TurnIndex)} + \CachedInputTokenPrice\, \CachedInputTokenCount^{(\TurnIndex)} + \OutputTokenPrice\, \OutputLength^{(\TurnIndex)} + \sum_{j} \ToolCallCost(\ExecutedToolCall_{t,j})\]

where input token categories are mutually disjoint, unit pricing matches token dimensions, and each tool execution charge \(\ToolCallCost\) is counted exactly once. This equation defines how in-flight reservations turn into settled charges against the budget.

Metering the append-only context workload of How multi-turn history drives up input cost against a full budget vector shows how a cache miss shifts which allowance dimension leads (notebook 10.3). The calculation assumes that the serving engine reuses KV-cache state for the system prompt and prior turns and bills each newly appended observation and tool output as uncached input. That reuse is not guaranteed by append-only text alone; the matching cache state must still be resident on the worker that serves the next call.

Napkin Math 10.3: Metering a trajectory against its budget
Problem: The coding workload has completed 30 turns under hypothetical allowances of 40 turns, 2 million effective input-plus-output tokens, 900 s elapsed, and $3.50. The clock reads 460 s. Assume all usage is settled, no paid-tool charges, and prices of $3 per million uncached input tokens, $0.30 per million cached input tokens, and $15 per million output tokens. Which allowance has the largest current used fraction in the reuse and no-reuse cases?

Math: With a base prompt of 8,000 tokens, 1,500 tokens appended per turn, and 200 output tokens per turn, equation 2.9 gives 892,500 input tokens over the trajectory, 898,500 with output. Under prefix reuse, every turn’s earlier prefix is billed as cached input, 841,000 tokens, so the model charge is $0.50. Billed entirely as uncached input, the same tokens cost $2.77.

Result: With reuse, the trajectory has used 75 percent of its turns, 44.9 percent of its tokens, 51.1 percent of its time, and 14.2 percent of its dollars, so turns lead. Without reuse, dollars lead at 79.1 percent.

Systems insight: The leading dimension changes when caching fails. The token meter counts cached input in full and cannot see the change; the dollar meter rises 5.5 times for the same tokens. A harness that sizes \(\CostCeiling\) from the cached bill will therefore meet its dollar ceiling first on exactly the trajectories that miss the cache. Reserving at the undiscounted rate and sizing the dollar allowance from the no-reuse cost keeps a cache miss from turning into a budget stop; what reuse is worth across a workload is priced in Pricing prefix cache hits.

10.9.4 Staged budget escalation and enforcement

If a trajectory runs at full scope until the admission gate abruptly refuses its next call, it often halts mid-investigation without compacting its findings, narrowing its tool use, or producing a useful diagnostic summary. A well-designed harness therefore introduces progressive interventions before the admission gate forcibly shuts down exploratory execution. Its primary warning signal is the maximum consumed fraction across all active budget dimensions, \(\max(\TurnBudgetUsedFraction, \TokenBudgetUsedFraction, \TimeBudgetUsedFraction, \CostBudgetUsedFraction)\), computed with unbilled reservations included rather than from settled invoices alone. The wall-clock timer yields the temporal fraction \(\TimeBudgetUsedFraction\). This multi-dimensional indicator steers adaptive interventions, even as the underlying gate continues to evaluate bounded liabilities independently across each axis.

Table 9 delineates the progressive escalation stages that precede hard enforcement. A deployment policy triggers each stage from the admission terms themselves rather than from a fixed percentage of the allowance, because a fixed percentage ignores how large the next step is. Steering begins when the remaining headroom can fund only a few more calls at their current bounded liability, and finalization begins when the next call’s liability no longer fits beside the finalization reserve. The stages change how a trajectory approaches a hard ceiling, not which stopping rule prevails; a hard ceiling still takes precedence over satisficing and marginal stopping, the order established for search in Reserving budget before admitting work.

Each intervention during the steering and restriction stages carries its own accounting and caching trade-offs. Context compaction can reclaim token headroom when sufficient budget remains to execute it without destroying critical state, although invoking a summarization model consumes additional tokens of its own and discards detail. Narrowing authority is similarly partial. Downgrading an agent to read-only tools prevents irreversible state mutations while still allowing expensive retrieval queries or context leaks to external APIs. Revoking delegation privileges stops new child trajectories without canceling background subtasks that are already active or releasing their reserved liabilities. Even how the harness enforces a tool restriction matters for cost, because dynamically mutating the tool schema inside the prompt invalidates cached prefix state from the modification point onward, which trades prefix-cache reuse for reduced tool exposure unless the restriction is enforced at the harness admission gate instead.

↩︎ The prompt reduction and information loss of each compaction method are established in Choosing a compaction method.

When a trajectory enters the finalization stage due to budget exhaustion, the system must not compromise its validation standards. If an expensive regression test remains in-flight, its reservation must remain held until final usage telemetry settles the charge. The final diagnostic report must accurately report candidate r18 and record which validation suites completed successfully, rather than treating an early unit test pass as substitute verification for incomplete end-to-end checks.

10.9.5 Choosing a ceiling from task evidence

Set a trajectory ceiling too low and the harness cuts off repairs that would have succeeded in a few more turns; set it too high and failed runs burn through the extra allowance without recovering. Enforcement makes a ceiling binding, so choosing its value requires evidence from representative tasks. Engineers evaluate candidate budget vectors by testing the end-to-end control policy across a workload sample while holding models, tool suites, and acceptance tests constant. The acceptance fraction, accepted tasks divided by all attempted tasks, counts both outright failures and trajectories halted by budget exhaustion. System evaluations must report total dollar expenditure, wall-clock latency, unresolved background liabilities, and operator intervention frequency alongside raw task completion. Dividing the total cost of all attempts by the accepted count gives the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2), which amortizes the expenses of failed and abandoned runs across successful completions. Budget limits appear artificially inexpensive whenever benchmark evaluations omit the costs of failed trajectories from total expenditure. Kapoor et al. (2024) argue that evaluating agent accuracy in isolation from cost produces needlessly fragile and expensive architectures, so the two must be optimized jointly.

Baseline profiling under unconstrained budgets reveals the empirical completion time and token distributions of successful trajectories, highlighting candidate threshold targets. However, because early steering, throttling, and finalization alter downstream model reasoning, engineers must evaluate the complete policy empirically rather than assuming post-hoc truncation models reality. In practice, empirical cost-benefit curves rarely display a sharp knee. The operational decision rule selects the budget ceiling that minimizes cost per accepted task while keeping unresolved liabilities and manual interventions within acceptable operational bounds. When the difference between two policies falls within measurement uncertainty, the simpler policy is preferred.

Baseline profiling of public repair-agent runs shows what such distributions look like. Figure 5 plots the distribution of model calls per trajectory for five published agent configurations on the same 500 repository-repair tasks (Jimenez et al. 2024), split by whether the patch was accepted.

Two lessons follow for ceiling choice. Failed runs take more calls than accepted ones in every configuration of figure 5, so the tail of the call distribution is mostly spend on attempts that will not be accepted. A ceiling at the accepted runs’ 90th percentile halts at most 10 percent of eventual successes but 15 to 31 percent of eventual failures. The same figure also warns against reading a ceiling off another configuration, because the percentile ranges from 46 to 101 calls across the five and differs even between the two agents built on the same model (82 and 101). These percentiles come from post-hoc truncations of unconstrained runs rather than enforced policy trials: because a ceiling the agent can see changes how it spends the remaining calls, the estimate locates a candidate ceiling and the policy evaluation confirms it.

Re-running the same model under several enforced ceilings supplies the confirmation. A computer-use benchmark reports 5 models at step limits of 15, 50, and 100 actions (Xie et al. 2024), plotted in figure 6.

Among the 5 models in figure 6, 3 gain nothing from the last 50 steps, while the strongest gains 20 points between the lowest and highest ceiling. A generous fixed ceiling therefore buys nothing for most configurations and costs the extra spend of every run that uses it, whereas a tight one forfeits real successes for the configurations that convert extra steps into progress.

↪ Matched-budget comparison of complete policies under one fixed resource envelope is developed in Matched-budget comparison.

Checkpoint 10.3: A budget is an admission decision

The repair trajectory is paused with an unresolved paid test job. A smaller model call would fit the currently unreserved allowance, but the task’s elapsed deadline has passed.

Bounded-liability admission completes the harness’s supervisory control plane: the trajectory record tracks ownership and outstanding obligations, guarded transitions govern state changes and waits, the scheduler divides shared model service, and multi-dimensional budgets cap lifetime spend without relying on model self-restraint. Resource accounting, however, bounds aggregate consumption rather than semantic problem-solving progress; catching a stalled repair loop before it exhausts its allowance requires synthesizing progress evidence across repeated tool failures and state transitions. Even for resource and lifecycle bounds, these guarantees hold only when engineers enforce them at the runtime boundary rather than substituting cooperative coroutines, settled-spend checks, or prompt instructions.

↪ Progress evidence for detecting a trajectory that loops without advancing is developed in Progress evidence.

10.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Architectural failures in agent systems rarely stem from exotic distributed bugs; they occur when engineers mistake a convenient implementation mechanism or an agreeable language model for an authoritative supervisory guarantee. The harness must enforce safety, resource bounds, and lifecycle invariants externally, regardless of how cooperative the model appears.

Fallacy: Using asynchronous coroutines is sufficient to build a harness.

Asynchronous coroutines and non-blocking I/O primitives yield worker threads during network calls, but cooperative concurrency is not a supervisory control plane. A coroutine lacks runtime-owned control state and guarded admission control. When a harness relies solely on naive coroutine scheduling and polls for operator signals only after a complete model turn returns, it surrenders control during inference. For an allowance of 4,096 tokens, an operator issuing a pause command is left waiting roughly 86.3 s, even at the queue-free reference generation rates analyzed in section 10.5. A harness keeps control state in an authoritative record that a pause updates at once, and it applies streaming cancellation: it stops waiting on the reply immediately and asks the serving engine to stop generating. The stop takes effect locally without waiting for the allowance to drain, while the call’s reservation stays outstanding until the service reports how many tokens it actually generated.

Pitfall: Checking only measured spend before dispatch.

Evaluating admission against settled accounting balances ignores both in-flight liabilities and terminal cleanup obligations. In notebook 10.2, the settled bill shows $0.92 of remaining headroom, making a new $0.21 call appear affordable. However, once the harness factors in the unsettled test reservation alongside the mandatory finalization reserve, the true available headroom shrinks to $0.12. Admitting that call overcommits the budget and leaves downstream finalization checks $0.09 short of their required funds. To prevent budget overruns, the runtime must enforce bounded-liability admission before dispatch under equation 1, reserving worst-case consumption upfront, settling against authoritative usage records upon completion, and ring-fencing funds for clean termination. Presenting the remaining balance to the model can assist its internal planning, but runtime admission control alone must provide the hard guarantee.

Fallacy: A parseable reply is dispatchable after cancellation.

When an invocation is canceled mid-stream due to a timeout or an operator signal, the incoming buffer may still contain syntactically valid fragments, such as a complete tool call formatted in JSON. Syntactic validity is merely a serialization property that confers neither execution authority nor semantic completeness. Under the quarantining rule, which applies invariant closure (principle 1) to the reply status, incomplete or aborted outputs must be isolated as diagnostic telemetry rather than forwarded to tool executors. Any subsequent action must originate from a fresh turn that passes the harness’s standard status and admission checks.

Pitfall: Treating a pause, timeout, or terminal state as settlement.

Stopping a wait after a timeout is not equivalent to settling the underlying resource obligation. Releasing a test job’s reservation upon a local timeout returns $0.50, which is 14.3 percent of the total trajectory allowance in notebook 10.2, to the uncommitted pool. If subsequent turns claim that capacity before the remote job finishes and reports its actual bill, the trajectory overshoots its budget ceiling by up to the reallocated amount. A correct settlement rule separates observation eligibility from settlement. When an operation times out, the harness revokes the agent’s eligibility to observe the late output, keeps its charges open in the operation table, and routes authenticated late evidence to the settlement owner rather than injecting it into an obsolete model turn.

An instruction honored on almost every turn still fails across a whole trajectory.

Fallacy: An instruction in the prompt is a runtime control.

Natural-language prompt instructions communicate semantic intent to the model, but they cannot enforce runtime security boundaries. A model that honors a freeze or budget constraint with probability 99 percent on any single turn sustains that compliance across a 30-turn trajectory only 74 percent of the time (equation 1.1). Consequently, 26 percent of executed trajectories violate the constraint at least once. The production outage in war story 10.1 shows the missing piece: the freeze existed only as prompt text, and nothing at the dispatch boundary enforced it. The war story does not report a turn count or a compliance rate, so it cannot show the compounding itself; the arithmetic shows why a freeze held that way fails in a predictable share of long runs. An authoritative policy guard must sit at the dispatch boundary, evaluating the proposed action deterministically on every turn regardless of what the prompt requested.

10.11 Summary

Because an agent loop possesses no intrinsic stopping condition, the decision to continue execution must rest with an external supervisor that the language model cannot manipulate or bypass. The agent harness serves this supervisory role. It reduces every operational decision to two invariants evaluated against a single, runtime-owned record. First, before initiating any new unit of work, the harness verifies whether active control signals, delegation grants, provider capacity, and reserved budget allowances still permit dispatch. Second, whenever an asynchronous operation completes or halts, the harness determines whether verified evidence has genuinely settled the in-flight liability, or whether the outstanding obligation still requires an owner. Operator pauses, human-in-the-loop approvals, fair-share scheduling, and hard budget ceilings are these two checks applied at different stages of the execution lifecycle.

The synchronization cost of this discipline is modest: a brief critical section to update the state machine and several accounting fields per transition. Conversely, omitting these harness boundaries introduces subtle failure modes that model-level metrics obscure. Prompt instructions that hold reliably on single turns compound into unacceptable failure rates over long horizons. Naive retrospective spend checks admit model invocations that deplete remaining funds mid-generation. Releasing budget reservations prematurely permits concurrent steps to overshoot cost ceilings. Table 10 summarizes the governing relations that regulate trajectory continuation, establish latency bounds under control signals, and allocate shared capacity across competing workloads.

Key Takeaways: Control lives outside the model
  • Instructions cannot substitute for architectural controls: A freeze directive honored on 99 percent of individual turns survives across a 30-turn trajectory only 74 percent of the time. In contrast, an invariant enforced at the dispatch boundary holds across every turn because the harness inspects the concrete operation rather than relying on model compliance.
  • Control signals must decouple from generation completion: Checking for pause or cancellation signals only after a generation finishes can delay a pause by approximately 86.3 s for a 4,096-token allowance, even under zero queueing. Turn boundaries combined with streaming cancellation decouple supervisory latency from generation length.
  • Halting execution does not settle in-flight obligations: A pause, timeout, or cancellation revokes an operation’s permission to advance the trajectory into the next turn, but it neither terminates external execution nor refunds committed spend. Only correlated, authenticated evidence from the remote provider can formally settle the liability; until that evidence arrives, the harness must preserve explicit ownership and state tracking for the outstanding task.
  • Asynchronous waits release execution threads, not system resources: Moving a trajectory into an asynchronous wait or human approval queue releases the active worker thread, but the trajectory retains proposal and evidence references, unresolved reservations, and its advancing deadline. Workspace and KV-cache residency continue only under their respective lease and cache policies. By Little’s law (\(\ApprovalHeldTrajectories = \ArrivalRate\,\ExternalWait\)), human review latency multiplies directly into the volume of concurrently suspended trajectories and their retained resource footprints.
  • Fairness, quota allocation, and budget affordability operate as independent gates: Scheduling weights allocate relative shares of provider capacity, meaning that request payload sizes rather than weights alone determine invocation frequency under deficit round-robin. Provider rate limits protect shared infrastructure against burst exhaustion, whereas task-level budget ceilings bound total lifetime consumption for an individual agent run.
  • Budget ceilings require upfront reservation of in-flight liabilities: A naive retrospective spend check reports $0.92 of available headroom, whereas bounded-liability admission, which counts unsettled in-flight tool executions and the finalization reserve, reveals only $0.12. Reserving conservative worst-case liabilities prior to dispatch is the only mechanism that converts an informational budget tracker into an enforceable upper bound.
  • Size budget ceilings by cost per accepted task: A restrictive budget ceiling appears deceptively economical if failed and exhausted runs are omitted from post hoc accounting. Sizing budget ceilings by effective cost per accepted task (\(\CostPerAcceptedTask = \mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]/\AcceptanceProbability\)), which amortizes failed attempts over accepted outcomes, and treating differences within measurement error as ties, aligns harness admission thresholds with production economics.

Trajectory-level encapsulation (principle 10) establishes the runtime-owned record as the authoritative foundation for supervision. By storing immutable references to external state rather than attempting to duplicate it, the harness decouples admission control and execution governance from model cooperation. Both responsibilities function within explicit resource and evidence boundaries, ensuring that every transition (a normal dispatch, a staged escalation, or an ungraceful abort) preserves an unambiguous owner for every effect still in flight.

10.12 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

An agent harness synthesizes classical ideas that operating systems, databases, and computer networks developed when shared infrastructure encountered clients that could not be trusted to self-regulate or terminate. In operating systems, Hoare introduced monitors to provide shared mutable state with a single serialized entry point (Hoare 1974). In reactive systems, Harel formalized statecharts with guarded transitions so that valid controller behavior could be rigorously defined and checked (Harel 1987). Saltzer and Schroeder articulated the foundational design principles of computer protection (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975), establishing the requirement of complete mediation: every access must pass through an authoritative check that the client cannot bypass. The trajectory record, its serialization lock, and its guarded transition table apply these three concepts directly to the execution of an autonomous agent run.

Kung, H. T., and John T. Robinson. 1981. “On Optimistic Methods for Concurrency Control.” ACM Transactions on Database Systems 6 (2): 213–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/319566.319567.
Gray, Cary G., and David R. Cheriton. 1989. “Leases: An Efficient Fault-Tolerant Mechanism for Distributed File Cache Consistency.” Proceedings of the Twelfth ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP), 202–10. https://doi.org/10.1145/74850.74870.

Concurrency control and resource accounting soon followed. Optimistic concurrency control (Kung and Robinson 1981) demonstrated that systems can validate updates against previously read versions rather than holding exclusive locks across slow, unpredictable external operations. The harness’s record_version check adopts this exact pattern, allowing an agent to plan or invoke external tools without blocking concurrent readers. O’Neil’s escrow method (O’Neil 1986) partitioned shared quantities by pre-allocating portions against an aggregate invariant that survives concurrent execution; its escrow is a reserved amount, the sense this chapter calls a reservation. It is the direct intellectual ancestor of bounded-liability admission, which admits an agent step only if the worst-case financial or token liability remains safe. Similarly, Gray and Cheriton introduced leases (Gray and Cheriton 1989) to ensure that distributed locks and allocated resources expire automatically if a client crashes or stalls, preventing abandoned runs from holding shared resources indefinitely.

Networking and distributed systems contributed mechanisms for queue management and fairness under contention. Demers, Keshav, and Shenker developed fair queueing (Demers et al. 1989), and Shreedhar and Varghese later introduced deficit round-robin (Shreedhar and Varghese 1995) to allocate link capacity equitably across flows of unequal packet size without expensive per-packet accounting. In an agent platform, where requests vary widely in token counts and latency, carried deficit credits prevent heavy agent runs from starving interactive workloads. Little’s law (Little 1961) relates the mean number of items waiting to their arrival rate and mean residence time, the relation needed to size these waiting queues. When agent steps involve slow tool execution or external inspection, staged event-driven architectures (Welsh et al. 2001) showed how to keep long waits from pinning execution threads by making queues explicit and enforcing localized admission controls at each processing stage.

Little, John D. C. 1961. “A Proof for the Queuing Formula: \(L = \lambda W\).” Operations Research 9 (3): 383–87. https://doi.org/10.1287/opre.9.3.383.
Parasuraman, Raja, Thomas B. Sheridan, and Christopher D. Wickens. 2000. “A Model for Types and Levels of Human Interaction with Automation.” IEEE Transactions on Systems, Man, and Cybernetics - Part A: Systems and Humans 30 (3): 286–97. https://doi.org/10.1109/3468.844354.

System safety also relies on decades of human-factors research into supervisory control. Bainbridge’s classic analysis of the ironies of automation (Bainbridge 1983) showed that humans perform poorly when tasked with passively monitoring highly reliable automated systems, precisely because human vigilance deteriorates when intervention is rarely needed. Parasuraman, Sheridan, and Wickens (Parasuraman et al. 2000) subsequently classified automation into explicit operational levels based on the degree to which an autonomous system decides and acts without human intervention. These insights explain why agent approval gates cannot rely on casual human oversight, but instead require explicit, structured pauses that supply unambiguous context before an action proceeds.

Modern language-model agents encountered all of these classical systems challenges simultaneously. Yao et al. (2023) framed agent execution as an interleaved loop of chain-of-thought reasoning and external tool actions with ReAct. Yang et al. (2024) brought this pattern into real software repositories with SWE-agent, demonstrating that purpose-built agent-computer interfaces could resolve real bugs, while also showing that execution cost, waiting latencies, and tool interruptions were no longer edge cases. The hazard of relying on prompt instructions alone became apparent when an autonomous agent deleted a production database during an instructed code freeze (Sharwood 2025), illustrating that a safety invariant stated only in natural language is not enforced. In parallel, systematic benchmarks that measure accuracy without tracking cost (Kapoor et al. 2024) obscure critical engineering trade-offs, which is why resource ceilings should be derived from measured cost distributions rather than optimistic assumptions. The harness developed in this chapter is the systems response to these operational pressures, enforcing classical guarantees directly at the agent trajectory.

Yao, Shunyu, Jeffrey Zhao, Dian Yu, Nan Du, Izhak Shafran, Karthik Narasimhan, and Yuan Cao. 2023. “ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Sharwood, Simon. 2025. Vibe Coding Service Replit Deleted User’s Production Database, Faked Data, Told Fibs Galore. The Register.
Kapoor, Sayash, Benedikt Stroebl, Zachary S. Siegel, Nitya Nadgir, and Arvind Narayanan. 2024. “AI Agents That Matter.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2407.01502.

For further reading:

  • Saltzer and Schroeder, “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems” (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975): foundational security principles, including complete mediation, that govern every admission check and authorization boundary in the harness.
  • Hoare, “Monitors: An Operating System Structuring Concept” (Hoare 1974): the classical formulation of serialized access to shared state, providing the conceptual model for trajectory record locking.
  • O’Neil, “The Escrow Transactional Method” (O’Neil 1986): reservations against an aggregate invariant under concurrency, the intellectual foundation for bounded-liability admission.
  • Shreedhar and Varghese, “Efficient Fair Queuing Using Deficit Round Robin” (Shreedhar and Varghese 1995): a short paper showing how carried credit enables \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) fair scheduling across work items of variable size.
  • Bainbridge, “Ironies of Automation” (Bainbridge 1983): a classic analysis of why human monitoring of reliable automation degrades, directly relevant to the design of human approval gates.
  • Yang et al., “SWE-agent: Agent-Computer Interfaces Enable Automated Software Engineering” (Yang et al. 2024): a concrete account of command-and-feedback loops in real software repositories, illustrating where model autonomy ends and harness infrastructure must take over.
Saltzer, J. H., and M. D. Schroeder. 1975. “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems.” Proceedings of the IEEE 63 (9): 1278–308. https://doi.org/10.1109/proc.1975.9939.
Yang, John, Carlos E Jimenez, Alexander Wettig, Kilian Lieret, Shunyu Yao, Karthik Narasimhan, and Ofir Press. 2024. “SWE-Agent: Agent-Computer Interfaces Enable Automated Software Engineering.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems.

10.13 Exercises

Every given below is a hypothetical problem input, not a measurement of a particular model, provider, or service. Prices are in dollars per million tokens unless stated otherwise. Each problem asks for a number and then for the decision the number supports.

  1. Where a pause lands (Basic). A repair harness checks pending_control only when a model reply ends. Generation takes 20 ms per output token with no queueing, and each call’s output allowance is \(\OutputLimit = 8{,}000\) tokens. The operator requirement is that a pause stop new admission within 15 s of its request.

    Compute the worst-case wait before the pause takes effect, and the largest \(\OutputLimit\) that meets the requirement without streaming cancellation. For this hypothetical problem, assume a 3,000-token patch can be partitioned into independently complete, validated edit proposals, each no larger than the reduced output cap. Their complete serialization totals 3,000 output tokens with no added proposal overhead, and each call carries the same 40,000-token context without a cache discount. If \(\OutputLimit\) is lowered to meet the requirement, what is the minimum number of complete calls, and how many extra input tokens, at $3 per million, does resending the context cost compared with one call? Explain why an indivisible proposal cannot instead be completed by concatenating truncated replies. The serving service acknowledges a streaming cancellation within 2 s. Decide between the lower cap and streaming cancellation, and state what the pause must still leave in the operation table for a test job already running.

  2. Admission after settlement (Basic). A repair trajectory has a $5.00 allowance, $4.05 of settled spend, a $0.25 reservation on an unsettled test job, and a $0.40 finalization reserve. The next model call carries 80,000 input tokens and \(\OutputLimit = 2{,}000\), at $2 per million input tokens and $10 per million output tokens.

    Use equation 1 to decide whether the call is admitted, and compare the spend-check headroom. The call settles with 20,000 uncached input tokens, 60,000 cached input tokens at $0.20 per million, and 900 output tokens. How much reservation does settlement release? The reply proposes paid tool calls bounded at $0.10 each; how many can the harness admit? A defective harness had instead released the test job’s reservation when its wait timed out. How many tool calls would it admit, and by how much would the finalization reserve fall short once the test’s full $0.25 charge arrives?

  3. Workspaces held in escrow (Basic). Review requests for repair submissions arrive at 12 per hour, and reviewers take 45 minutes on average to decide. Each held trajectory keeps a workspace lease of 4 GB, and the pool reserves 24 GB for held workspaces.

    Use Little’s law to compute the mean number of held trajectories and the memory they retain. What mean decision time would fit the reserved pool? An alternative snapshots each workspace to storage on entering AWAITING_APPROVAL, keeping 0.25 GB resident, and restores it in 40 s at dispatch. Compute its resident memory and the restore delay as a fraction of the mean decision time. Recommend one design, and explain why a pool sized from the mean alone would still fail after an incident that produces a burst of review requests.

  4. Three classes under deficit round-robin (Intermediate). A harness schedules three classes with quantum 10,000 charge tokens per unit weight per round: support calls (weight 4; 3,000 input tokens and a 500-token output allowance), repair calls (weight 2; 30,000 and 4,000), and maintenance calls (weight 1; 60,000 and 4,000). Assume all classes remain continuously backlogged at the stated call sizes, no provider or local-capacity gate binds, and carried credit has a ceiling large enough for the largest permitted call. One round takes 2 s.

    Compute each class’s share of the scheduling charge, its long-run calls per round, and the rounds of credit one call needs. What is the mean interval between maintenance-class admissions in seconds? An engineer caps every class’s carried credit at 50,000 tokens to limit bursts. Determine which class stops running and why. To increase maintenance-class admission frequency, one proposal raises the maintenance weight to 3; another compacts maintenance inputs to 20,000 tokens. Compute the rounds per call and the support class’s charge share under each proposal, and choose the one that leaves support service unchanged.

  5. Sizing a dollar ceiling for cache misses (Intermediate). A repair trajectory runs 25 turns with a 10,000-token base prompt, 2,000 tokens appended per turn, and 300 output tokens per turn; each call’s output allowance is 4,000 tokens. Prices are $3 per million uncached input tokens, $0.30 per million cached input tokens, and $15 per million output tokens, and each turn’s earlier prefix is billed as cached input.

    Use equation 2.9 to compute the total input tokens, and the settled cost with and without prefix reuse. The team set \(\CostCeiling = \$1.00\) from the cached bill with a $0.15 finalization reserve, and the harness reserves each call at the undiscounted input rate and full output allowance. After 24 cached turns, is the 25th call admitted, and with what headroom? A long approval wait evicts the cache before one turn, so that turn’s whole input is billed uncached and later turns hit the cache again. Compute the extra charge for an eviction before the 6th turn and before the 24th turn, and state how many of each the trajectory can absorb before the 25th call is refused. What \(\CostCeiling\) guarantees admission of all 25 calls if every turn misses? Recommend how to size the ceiling.

  6. Which allowance binds the research agent (Intermediate). The research agent starts from a 6,000-token base prompt, appends 12,000 tokens of bounded page observations per turn, and generates 400 output tokens per turn with a 2,000-token output allowance. Each turn takes 45 s. Its budget is \(\TrajectoryBudget = \langle 40 \text{ turns},\ 1.5 \text{ million tokens},\ 1{,}800 \text{ s},\ \$3.00 \rangle\), where the token meter counts cached input in full, and prices match problem 5.

    Admitting a turn requires its input plus its output allowance to fit the remaining token allowance. Find the last admitted turn, and compute the used fraction of every dimension at that point with prefix reuse. Which allowance binds, and how does that differ from the repair trajectory of notebook 10.3? A compaction policy runs after every fifth turn: a compaction call reads the full context and writes 2,000 tokens, and the context then restarts from the base prompt plus an 8,000-token summary. Find how many turns the agent now completes before a turn or compaction is refused. Compare this with the token allowance needed to reach 30 turns without compaction. Decide between compaction and a larger \(\TrajectoryTokenLimit\), naming the evidence a summary of untrusted pages must keep by reference.

  7. Choosing a ceiling from a trial (Advanced). A generous-budget run of 100 repair tasks, capped at $10, gives the following outcomes: 30 tasks accepted at about $0.50 each, 25 at about $1.50, 15 at about $3.00, and 5 at about $6.00; the remaining 25 never reached acceptance and spent the full $10. Assume that under a lower cap a task that the generous run accepted at or below the cap is still accepted at the same cost, and every other task spends exactly the cap.

    Compute the accepted count and cost per accepted task (equation 1.2) for caps of $1, $2, $4, and $10. A colleague proposes $10 because it maximizes acceptance; critique the proposal. Treating the acceptance count as binomial and freezing each policy’s total cost, estimate the marginal relative standard error of \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) at each cap. Use the first-order approximation that the relative standard error of inverse acceptance equals the acceptance-rate standard error divided by the estimated acceptance fraction. Is the gap between the two lowest-cost caps large compared with each policy’s marginal error? State which paired cost and outcome records, and which declared comparison criterion, a formal verdict would require; these marginal errors alone do not establish distinguishability. How many tasks would bring the relative error to 3 percent at the $2 cap’s acceptance fraction? Finally, explain why a policy that steers or compacts near its cap must be tested as a policy, and why these numbers cannot simply be read from the generous run for it.

  8. Settling timed-out test jobs (Advanced). Each repair trajectory dispatches six test jobs to a metered runner that charges $0.01 per second and enforces a 90 s cap. The harness waits 30 s per job, and 70 percent of jobs finish within the wait at an average of 20 s; the rest run on, averaging 60 s in total. The trajectory allowance is \(\CostCeiling = \$4.00\). The current harness releases a job’s whole reservation when its wait times out and books nothing until the charge arrives.

    Compute each job’s bounded liability, the expected late charge per trajectory that the defective harness leaves uncovered, the worst case, and both as fractions of \(\CostCeiling\). Under the settlement rule, how much allowance does a timed-out job hold beyond its eventual charge, on average, while it runs? The runner also supports confirmed cancellation, which stops a job within 5 s and reports its final usage. Compute the charge and the released reservation for a job canceled at its timeout, and the expected test cost per trajectory under “cancel at timeout” and “let it run.” Redesign the timeout policy for the repair agent, stating which evidence releases each reservation and what a canceled job’s missing result costs the repair.

What’s Next: From a live record to a durable one
If the host fails after reserving and dispatching the test job but before recording its response, the live record alone cannot preserve the admitted operation, its reservation, the held approval, or the evidence needed to resume. Durable Execution develops the history and ordering needed to reconstruct selected control state and distinguish recorded outcomes from unresolved operations after a crash. Rebuilding the record does not itself prove that a remote effect failed, so resumption must preserve the tool’s settlement contract rather than blindly repeat the call.
